# Evidence corpus — 47-source controlled derivation

**Status:** DERIVED — originals and public source representations remain controlling
**Generated:** 13 August 2026
**Generation method:** `scripts/build_evidence_corpus.py`
**Normalization:** HTML markup removed, entities decoded and terminal whitespace removed for display; spelling, syntax, punctuation, OCR damage and redaction tokens otherwise preserved. No silent clinical correction.
**Ordering:** dated records first by recorded date; records or sections without an independently mapped date follow as `UNMAPPED`, ordered by stable Source ID and repository line.
**Inputs and hashes:** `sources/manifest/source-manifest.csv`
**Controlling rule:** this corpus never replaces the native exhibit or its public repository representation.

# 6 July 2017 — GP Podiatry referral / prioritisation note

[GP17POD-20170706-0003]
**Source type:** primary contemporaneous GP clinical record

[GP17POD-20170706-0004]
**Practice:** Galway East Medical Practice

[GP17POD-20170706-0005]
**Date:** 6 July 2017

[GP17POD-20170706-0006]
**Provenance:** extracted from the 2017 GP record PDF supplied directly to the project on 12 August 2026.

[GP17POD-20170706-0007]
**Status:** faithful transcription of the relevant entry. The original PDF remains controlling. No analytical findings are made on this page.

[GP17POD-20170706-0009]
---

[GP17POD-20170706-0011]
> **06/07/2017 A66: Referral to other provider/nurse/therapist/social worker - Phoned podiatry to discuss case - will mark as priority case and as Parkinson's - should be called mid September**

[GP17POD-20170706-0013]
---

[GP17POD-20170706-0015]
## Source-status note

[GP17POD-20170706-0017]
This entry directly establishes that on 6 July 2017 the GP contacted Podiatry about Liam's case and recorded that the case would be marked as a priority **“as Parkinson's.”**

[GP17POD-20170706-0019]
It does not by itself establish:

[GP17POD-20170706-0021]
- the exact wording of any written referral;

[GP17POD-20170706-0022]
- what clinical detail Podiatry received beyond the telephone discussion;

[GP17POD-20170706-0023]
- whether Podiatry understood its role as adjunctive structural care, principal explanation of the foot problem, or something else;

[GP17POD-20170706-0024]
- what treatment was later provided or what information returned to Neurology or the GP.

[GP17POD-20170706-0026]
Those remain questions for the original Podiatry referral and pathway records.

> L17: FOOT/TOE SYMPTOM/COMPLAINT - C/o abnormal gait and foot
> pain bilat. says feels gait started due to sciatica issue left leg years ago
> and now might be more "habitual" as no pain in back or leg at present.
> Works on feet and finding it tough. Smokes 10/day but marked tar
> staining fingers. On examination marked mallet toes, onychogryphosis
> and callouses/pressure areas. Plan referral podiatry clinic but also
> bloods diabetes screen and U&E, LFTs, CRP. Await old notes
> regarding past history as quite unkempt and ?element other issues
> ongoing here.

> A62: Administrative procedure - Looking for another cert
> Has been off for three weeks already
> First week was off because of a cough

> sensations in head like electric shocks
> Tried taking antibiotics for flu like illness but felt they made him feel
> worse so stopped them
> main issues now are the ongoing clamminess/sweating, headaches and
> no energy at all
> no night sweats
> no rashes
> still coughing a bit - can be productive
> no haemoptysis
> no chest pain
> not in a relationship

>

## N17 — Neurology notes 2017

[N17-20170713-0007]
ADDENDUM at Transcription finalisation 2017-07-13th_21:41hrs

[N17-20170713-0008]
Dear [GP], (Dear [Patient])

[N17-20170713-0009]
I do apologise for the delay in getting this out as I’ve been on Consults for the last two

[N17-20170713-0010]
weeks

[N17-20170713-0011]
However , the DATSCAN is now done:

[N17-20170713-0012]
[Patient]’s DATSCAN is now completed from earlier today.

[N17-20170713-0013]
It is abnormal as was expected, with marked findings in both basal ganglia.

[N17-20170713-0014]
The report says Parkinson’s Disease or Lewy Body disease - - - however this is obviously

[N17-20170713-0015]
not LBD.

## HEALY17 — Healy response

[HEALY17-20170912-0001]
# 12 September 2017 — Prof Daniel G. Healy response after specialist review

[HEALY17-20170912-0003]
**Source type:** primary contemporaneous specialist correspondence

[HEALY17-20170912-0004]
**Author:** Prof Daniel G. Healy, Consultant Neurologist

[HEALY17-20170912-0005]
**Recipient:** Dr Thomas Monaghan, Consultant Neurologist, Galway University Hospital

[HEALY17-20170912-0006]
**Clinic date:** 12 September 2017

[HEALY17-20170912-0007]
**Provenance:** photographed signed/typed letter supplied directly by Liam Moloney to the project on 12 August 2026 and visually inspected.

[HEALY17-20170912-0008]
**Status:** redacted partial transcription of the clearly legible substantive passages. The photographed original remains controlling. No analytical findings are made on this page.

[HEALY17-20170912-0010]
---

[HEALY17-20170912-0012]
Dear Tom,

[HEALY17-20170912-0014]
> “Thank you for asking me to see this man who I think probably has Park II and I would suggest testing that initially. If it is negative I would go on to test a panel.”

[HEALY17-20170912-0016]
> “It is a little bit unclear but he probably developed Parkinsonism somewhere between his late 20s and early 30s.”

[HEALY17-20170912-0018]
The letter then discusses background/family-history considerations and possible heterozygous manifestations. Some words in that paragraph are not reproduced here because the supplied image is not sufficiently clear to guarantee a literal transcription of every word.

[HEALY17-20170912-0020]
> “There are very few non motor symptoms. He was able to detect 7/10 smells on the UPSIT scale. He describes a dramatic improvement in his motor symptoms with Procyclidine low dose qds and Rasagiline 1mg od. He takes Clonazepam at night.”

[HEALY17-20170912-0022]
Prof Healy records review of the MRI brain and advises keeping an open mind about a subtle imaging point if PARK II testing is negative. The exact wording of that imaging sentence should be taken from the original image rather than this partial transcript.

[HEALY17-20170912-0024]
> “He had a lot of questions which I answered to the best of my ability.”

[HEALY17-20170912-0026]
> “Physical activity would be helpful and as you are aware patients with PARK II tend to manage quite well for long periods of time with relatively low doses of Dopamine replacement therapy. They can become quite dyskinetic if over-used. One should mentally think of it as a nigropathy.”

[HEALY17-20170912-0028]
> “I have not arranged to see him again. I have given him his CDs back. I would be very interested in the results of the tests and happy to see in the future as required.”

[HEALY17-20170912-0030]
Yours sincerely,

[HEALY17-20170912-0032]
Prof. Daniel G. Healy

[HEALY17-20170912-0033]
Consultant Neurologist

[HEALY17-20170912-0035]
---

[HEALY17-20170912-0037]
## Source-status note

[HEALY17-20170912-0039]
This letter directly establishes what Prof Healy himself recorded after seeing Liam in September 2017. In particular it directly records:

[HEALY17-20170912-0041]
- Healy's opinion that Liam **“probably has Park II”**;

[HEALY17-20170912-0042]
- his recommendation to test that initially and move to a panel if negative;

[HEALY17-20170912-0043]
- his estimate that Parkinsonism probably developed between the late 20s and early 30s;

[HEALY17-20170912-0044]
- the reported dramatic motor improvement with Procyclidine and Rasagiline;

[HEALY17-20170912-0045]
- his statement that PARK II patients can manage for long periods on relatively low doses of dopamine replacement therapy while becoming dyskinetic if over-used;

[HEALY17-20170912-0046]
- his interest in the test results and willingness to review again if required.

[HEALY17-20170912-0048]
This source does **not** state that Prof Healy ordered immediate commencement of levodopa. It also does not by itself establish what Galway subsequently did with the advice. Those are separate analytical questions.

## TREMAIL17 — Contemporaneous treatment-response email compilation

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0001]
# Contemporaneous treatment-response emails — October 2017

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0003]
## Purpose

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0005]
Preserve contemporaneous patient-origin evidence about the effect of the neurological treatment regime before formal propositions are frozen.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0007]
This file distinguishes:

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0009]
- what Liam wrote contemporaneously in October 2017;

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0010]
- what Liam now recalls about the same episode;

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0011]
- what can safely be inferred;

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0012]
- what still requires the clinical record or expert interpretation.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0014]
---

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0016]
## 1. 4 October 2017 email — direct contemporaneous account during Kemadrin/procyclidine withdrawal

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0018]
**Source:** Gmail, sent by Liam Moloney to Celia Moloney, subject `Here`, 4 October 2017.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0020]
The email records Monaghan's staged Kemadrin/procyclidine titration and a planned treatment break to assess its effects. Liam wrote that within days of beginning half a tablet there had been significant improvement and described the period as `living in a miracle, literally`.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0022]
He listed concrete functional changes:

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0024]
- `My walk is back together.`

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0025]
- `My writing is good.`

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0026]
- `I can stand up straight.`

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0027]
- `My legs are loose and limber.`

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0028]
- he could stand while the bus was moving;

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0029]
- lift his bicycle without falling over it;

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0030]
- jump, run, hop and skip;

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0031]
- and perform ordinary dexterity tasks that had previously been difficult.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0033]
Four days into the Kemadrin break, he wrote:

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0035]
> `I am feeling the tension building up, my feet are restless and my walk is falling apart....but my eyesight is back to normal.`

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0037]
The same email also contains Liam's then-current historical reconstruction of the longstanding foot problem, including recurrent stinging on the outside of the left foot and years of severe burning foot pain. Importantly, it distinguishes foot pain from leg tension in his own experience while also trying to understand their place within the wider Parkinsonian illness.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0039]
### Source-status

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0041]
This is contemporaneous patient evidence, written while the withdrawal experiment was actually occurring. It directly supports that:

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0043]
- Kemadrin/procyclidine had produced marked global physical improvement;

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0044]
- a planned withdrawal was being used to assess the medication's effects;

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0045]
- during withdrawal, tension increased, the feet became restless and gait deteriorated;

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0046]
- visual side effects were simultaneously improving off the medication.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0048]
It does **not**, by itself, prove the mechanism of the foot symptoms or that levodopa would have produced the same effect.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0050]
---

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0052]
## 2. 24 October 2017 HR email — independent contemporaneous functional context

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0054]
**Source:** Gmail, sent by Liam Moloney to Pauline McDonagh and Deborah Walsh, subject `Re: Medical Assessment`, 24 October 2017.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0056]
Liam told HR that he had had to stop the medication about three or four weeks earlier because it was affecting his eyesight; the Parkinson's nurse took him off it for about 12 days and reintroduced it at a lower dosage. He reported that the lower dose did not cover all symptoms and that the nurse had just advised increasing the dose again while monitoring vision.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0058]
The email records:

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0060]
> `apart from the vision, I am physically very much improved, you wouldn't believe there was ever much wrong with me.`

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0062]
and:

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0064]
> `The joy of being able to move about normally and function unchallenged has been the equivalent of living in a miracle.`

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0066]
At the same time Liam recorded that he had a Podiatry appointment on Thursday, Physiotherapy on Friday and the Parkinson's nurse on Monday.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0068]
### Source-status

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0070]
This email corroborates, to a workplace recipient and for a practical occupational purpose, that the neurological medication regime had produced a profound physical improvement by October 2017, while visual toxicity was forcing withdrawal/reduction/re-escalation decisions.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0072]
It is particularly useful because it was not written for litigation, complaint or retrospective reconstruction.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0074]
---

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0076]
## 3. Present patient recollection — phenomenological clarification

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0078]
Liam now clarifies that the improvement during procyclidine titration felt like a profound release of longstanding tension that had become almost a constant background `sound` in his bodily experience. He recalls being able to `walk with power` and experiencing the improvement as almost perfect apart from residual issues such as hand cramps and visual disturbance.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0080]
He further recalls that when procyclidine was withdrawn, the **first pain to return** was the focal pain on the outside of the left foot, the same pain he dates back to 2004, and that this focal pain persisted until March 2025.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0082]
### Source-status

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0084]
The present recollection adds phenomenological detail not fully expressed in the surviving October emails. In particular, the claim that the outside-left-foot pain was the *first* symptom to return should remain tagged as present patient recollection unless and until a contemporaneous clinical note, email or witness source independently confirms that precise sequence.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0086]
The contemporaneous October emails do strongly support the broader state: dramatic improvement on Kemadrin/procyclidine, planned withdrawal to test effect, deterioration during withdrawal, foot restlessness during withdrawal, and re-escalation because the lower dose failed to control all symptoms.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0088]
---

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0090]
## 4. Clinical significance to be tested, not assumed

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0092]
The clean documentary point is not that procyclidine proved the feet were dystonia and not that procyclidine and levodopa are interchangeable.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0094]
It is narrower:

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0096]
> **Neurology's own treatment regime materially altered the patient's physical state, and the contemporaneous withdrawal experiment produced deterioration that included the feet.**

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0098]
Once that information existed, the neurological significance of treatment effects in the feet became a question requiring clinical interpretation.

[TREMAIL17-20171004-0100]
The later record should therefore be examined for whether Neurology documented what the medication-foot interaction meant, whether it changed the earlier Podiatry/Neurology allocation, and whether it entered the criteria governing prolonged dopaminergic deferral.

## MAIL17 — Patient medical-assessment email

[MAIL17-20171024-0001]
# 24 October 2017 — Re: Medical Assessment

[MAIL17-20171024-0003]
**Source type:** primary contemporaneous patient email

[MAIL17-20171024-0004]
**Author:** Liam Moloney

[MAIL17-20171024-0005]
**Recipients:** employer HR recipients (names retained in original Gmail; redacted here as non-essential)

[MAIL17-20171024-0006]
**Date/time:** 24 October 2017

[MAIL17-20171024-0007]
**Gmail message ID:** `15f4eacd53c5073c`

[MAIL17-20171024-0008]
**Provenance:** read directly from the connected Gmail account. This page is a redacted transcription of the patient-authored message body. No analytical findings are made on this page.

[MAIL17-20171024-0010]
---

[MAIL17-20171024-0012]
Hi Pauline,

[MAIL17-20171024-0014]
How are you? That date and time is fine by me to see the doctor.

[MAIL17-20171024-0016]
By way of query I wonder would you be able to outline the process from here on?

[MAIL17-20171024-0018]
I suppose I should give you a brief update as to where things are for me at the moment.

[MAIL17-20171024-0020]
You may already know that I had to stop taking my medication about three or four weeks ago.

[MAIL17-20171024-0022]
The particular meds that I was on were eating away at my eyesight, a bit startling it was.

[MAIL17-20171024-0024]
So the Parkinson's nurse took me off it for about 12 days and then reintroduced at a lower dosage.

[MAIL17-20171024-0026]
Initially the eyesight returned to normal. Then when I started back on the medication even the lower dose was playing with my vision.

[MAIL17-20171024-0027]
So I started wearing glasses. That is about two weeks ago.

[MAIL17-20171024-0029]
Unfortunately the lower dose doesn't tick off all of the symptoms so I need the higher dose, or some compromise of sorts. So the Nurse just rang me now and has said to rise the dose, keep wearing the glasses and get back to him on Monday coming and he will assess things then.

[MAIL17-20171024-0031]
So that's this moment in time.

[MAIL17-20171024-0032]
I have a podiatry appointment on Thurs morning, Physio Friday morning and the nurse on Monday then...kept moving anyway!!

[MAIL17-20171024-0034]
On the upshot, apart from the vision, I am physically very much improved, you wouldn't believe there was ever much wrong with me. The joy of being able to move about normally and function unchallenged has been the equivalent of living in a miracle.

[MAIL17-20171024-0036]
One concern I have is whether or not I will have to wear glasses all the time because this will mean I need my glasses fitted with protection in the cleanroom. I think the company do this but you may know more...

[MAIL17-20171024-0037]
And that's about it for the moment.

[MAIL17-20171024-0039]
I'll be there at 2.44 on the 3rd November to see the Doc.

[MAIL17-20171024-0040]
Hope to here from you meanwhile,

[MAIL17-20171024-0042]
Kind regards,

[MAIL17-20171024-0043]
Liam

[MAIL17-20171024-0045]
---

[MAIL17-20171024-0047]
**Redaction / scope note:** The original Gmail message includes the quoted prior HR scheduling email and corporate confidentiality footer. Those are not reproduced here because they contain third-party contact details and add no patient-authored medical content. The original message remains the controlling source.

## N18 — Neurology notes 2018

[N18-20180424-0007]
April 2018-04-24th

[N18-20180424-0008]
[Patient] finds concentration is good. He can play at a really, really high level “Countdown” on an ongoing basis. He continues doing this online - as the best over-30 player online, in the world

[N18-20180424-0009]
But he’ll very “easily loose train of thought and when the concentration is gone - - it’s gone”.

[N18-20180424-0010]
[Patient]’s main concerns are physical:

[N18-20180424-0011]
He is having foot cramps and hand cramps. It is nearly always the LEFT hand.

[N18-20180424-0012]
Two weeks ago , while walking up the stairs, he pulled a muscle in the RIGHT chest.

[N18-20180424-0013]
He has had a crick in the neck and shoulder e.g. on stretching to look for a car parking space.

[N18-20180424-0014]
The pain and discomfort is worse than he’d expect.

[N18-20180424-0015]
He knows that this should only last a day or two – but it can get to him for c 2-3 weeks. It is a “ball of pain” that moves.

[N18-20180424-0016]
[Patient] is very cold sensitive.

[N18-20180424-0017]
Hand swing is good.

[N18-20180424-0018]
He feels that the gait is a bit off – he lacks both “fluency and speed”, for someone who he feels was good beforehand.

[N18-20180424-0019]
The relief from tension is fantastic “It’s nice to be able to walk 20metres” as he says “without tension”.

[N18-20180424-0020]
Transitions on floor surfaces are now OK e.g. going from carpet to tiles.

[N18-20180424-0021]
Sleep is good.

[N18-20180424-0022]
Movement in bed is as good as ever it was.

[N18-20180424-0023]
Anxiety has subsided.

[N18-20180424-0024]
Toe supports on both feet are a help .

[N18-20180424-0025]
Nails are pared back.

[N18-20180424-0026]
Bowels are “right as rain”. Movements are fairly uniform and they are formed - - he is NOT having to push.

[N18-20180424-0027]
Two-and-a-half weeks ago he went to his general practitioner with a chest infection or at least a cough of 2 different semiologies. He was given reflux medications at that stage.

[N18-20180424-0028]
[Movement Disorder Specialist] had explained why he would probably find it easier to stop smoking than other people. The smoking is 11-12 per day and he knows we would be very keen on him trying to stop.

[N18-20180424-0029]
Vision is “excellent”, except – it’s more work than it used to be - - it’s like he has focus and concentrate.

[N18-20180424-0030]
He put on weight - - - but he is not bothered about this. I was a bit bothered though initially, but it is very stable and he’s happier at this level of weight currently.

[N18-20180424-0031]
Rivotril - - he has pains in upper back and pains in the neck - - - He doesn't know the dose but by the description I think it is 0.5 mg.

[N18-20180424-0032]
He would get 14 tablets a month and [Patient] will take a half tablet - - - he’s had 35 in the last 9 months – he will take a half of the 0.5mg. He’ll sometimes use it for sleep. It really helps the neck and back by the morning – and he’ll be better the next day for having taken it the night before.

[N18-20180424-0033]
===============

[N18-20180424-0034]
Our Nurse [Parkinson's Nurse] had suggested stopping Procyclidne for two weeks in Sept /Oct 2017 (regarding the blurring of the vision – [Patient] had seen me that day), but when he did that - - the first thing he noticed changing and changing rapidly was the return on the foot, of the outside left corn.

[N18-20180424-0035]
Symptoms got worse a bit.

[N18-20180424-0036]
(He remains on the Azilect)

[N18-20180424-0037]
But he was more “shifty” “unrelaxed” off the Proclyidine,

[N18-20180424-0038]
He had been off of it for a full two weeks.

[N18-20180424-0039]
He returned to Ailect at 2.5mg TDS-QDS

[N18-20180424-0040]
---------------------------

[N18-20180424-0041]
I’m delighted to note he is back at work in Shipping with Integer Medical. He feels he’s doing well with the physical task but also in a partial Health and Safety role too, and the cognitive aspects of it.

[N18-20180424-0042]
He summarises it in one important statement – “I enjoy being alive.”

[N18-20180424-0043]
He feels he does have a bit of irritability – (but he seems fine to me.)

[N18-20180424-0044]
[Patient] has some probably piriformis pain on the left side. It’s about 1x per week for a couple of hours.

[N18-20180424-0045]
He does vary the Procylidine a bit, this is unintentionally – and if he does miss it he will feel tighter in the left bottom/? Piriformis later.

[N18-20180424-0046]
.================

[N18-20180424-0047]
Current Rx:

[N18-20180424-0048]
1 x 1mg Azilect

[N18-20180424-0049]
4 x ½ half tablets of Procylidine i.e ½ of the 5mg = 2.5mg QDS (he is varying this a bit)

[N18-20180424-0050]
Co Q10 - - - he takes about 200mg per day.

[N18-20180424-0051]
Folate 5mg

[N18-20180424-0052]
Vit D.

[N18-20180424-0053]
Vit B

[N18-20180424-0054]
Rivotril - - he has pains in upper back and pains in the neck - - - He doesn't know the dose but by the description I think it is 0.5 mg.

[N18-20180424-0055]
He would get 14 tablets a month and [Patient] will take a half tablet - - - he’s had 35 in the last 9 months – he will take a half of the 0.5mg. He’ll sometimes use it for sleep. It really helps the neck in /back by the morning.

[N18-20180424-0056]
He is off the PPi currently – it didn’t anything much for the recent cough.

[N18-20180424-0057]
===============

[N18-20180424-0058]
Exam note:

[N18-20180424-0059]
[Patient] actually tends to move a little while he is sitting in the chair. It is not dyskinetic as such-and it is more than would be customarily expected in the hypokinetic disorder that it iPD. Obviously it is probably much better than him being frozen and bradykinetic. I certainly don't think this is an " overtreatment" phenomenology" in that it reflects the improvement in tension that he describes, and [Patient] describes that he was "always fidgety".

[N18-20180424-0060]
We did a video of him today both of the hand movements and also the gait.

[N18-20180424-0061]
There is bradykinesia however it is not bad. The walk is actually very good. He has a good arm swing without having to concentrate on it at all. Certainly the left-hand occasionally does "collide" with the leg but it is really relatively excellent.

[N18-20180424-0062]
================

[N18-20180424-0063]
Impression

[N18-20180424-0064]
Obviously he could be very much better if we would actually give him the levodopa or another dopamine agonist ---however for a very clearly set out reasons, we do not want to do that – at least for quite some time.

[N18-20180424-0065]
He probably isn't going to be very tolerant of being given more and more procyclidine given the visual issues previously with it.

[N18-20180424-0066]
He is still keen that we might at some stage increase the Procyclidine and we will try sooner or later - - probably increasing one timing at a time.

[N18-20180424-0067]
Incidentally, while our overarching interest with procyclidine is to avoid visual issues or cogntive difficulties, he was asking me as regards whether there will be cognitive consequences from being on the procyclidine? In point of fact if there are cognitive effects from procyclidine (and younger people tolerate it generally very well), they are pure "acute or transient effects" i.e. it is not doing any cognitive damage to him whatsoever.

[N18-20180424-0068]
As regards the Rivotril, in other circumstances we would be deeply concerned about this. However it is relatively infrequent and he is not taking an excessive amount of it. It is relatively long acting and therefore it has are much less addictive potential. It controls very well aspect of his Parkinsons  There is another particular justification for this:

[N18-20180424-0069]
It relates to the pains and cramps and spasms and that "ball of pain" in the neck that can go down the back.

[N18-20180424-0070]
[Patient] will have the ordinary aches and pains and “cricks” that all 40-year-olds have. However in the last 10 years it has begun to be significantly understood that people with Parkinson’s have a much more "magnified" experience of pain than everybody else.

[N18-20180424-0071]
Therefore what he describes is not unreasonable or unusual - although it sounds different to lots of other 40-year-olds, who might have some degree of degenerative disc changes: Because the Rivotril relaxes this to a certain degree, I think it is reasonable to let him have it in much the way he is taking.

[N18-20180424-0072]
In simple terms we are much more willing to tolerate in him then we will are in other people. The simple reason for this is because cognitively even though he describes how he can lose his train of thought, he is functioning at a particularly very high level.

[N18-20180424-0073]
I was initially a bit concerned about the weight but he is not worried. I would just ask them to keep a weekly record of the weight because I do not want it heavier than he is at the moment.

[N18-20180424-0074]
Recommendations

[N18-20180424-0075]
As detailed above  (I forgot to discuss running the Genetics with [Patient] today I do apologise --- – but he’ll have a copy of this at home and I’ll explain everything here:

[N18-20180424-0076]
Obviously I’d be particularly interested to know – but this is certainly not about satisfying my wanting to know: I sort of want to delay it - - but basically if at any time he wants [Patient] done – for any reason - we will run it. The logic of delaying is as follows:  It doesn’t particularly change current management – so there is no great loss in “not knowing” currently right now. I’d probably prefer run a Whole Genome Sequence in time to come, as opposed to the 30 gene panel or the Specific PARK 2 PRKN which we/Prof Healy suspect is the cause – which is what we might do now. I think if we leave it longer - - - and if we were having to be doing WGS (assuming negative PARK2 /Panel) that there will be a more evolved library of associated genes known of by then, rather than if we do it now.  But there is a reasonable logic to doing the isolated test for PARK2 PRKN/or the 30 gene panel now (and indeed Prof Healy would be happy with that approach) , if [Patient] would like? If he decides he wants it done at any time, if [Patient] might send me or [Parkinson's Nurse] a note – we’ll get it arranged.

1 x 1mg Azilect
