5mg tablets of Procylidine first thing in morning and then ½ of the 5mg = 2.5mg TDS (he is varying this a bit) he tried coming off this in Sept 2018 and was fully off it. He is back on it since early Jan 2019. He isn’t sure whether it did a lot in terms of coming back onto Procylidine but see text.

[N19-20190228-0144]
Co Q10 - - - he takes about 1x 200mg per day.

[N19-20190228-0145]
Folate 5mg

[N19-20190228-0146]
Vit D 2x 800 per day.

[N19-20190228-0147]
Vit B Complex from Holland and Barrett

[N19-20190228-0148]
Rivotril - - he has pains in upper back and pains in the neck

[N19-20190228-0149]
- - - By 2018 - - - He doesn't know the dose but by the description I think it is 0.5 mg.

[N19-20190228-0150]
He would get 14 tablets a month and [Patient] will take a half tablet - - - he’s had 35 in the last 9 months – he will take a half of the 0.5mg. He’ll sometimes use it for sleep. It really helps the neck in /back by the morning.  - - - By 2019 he had taken about 1 x 0.5mg tablet in the last three months. He has recently in 2019 been wondering about maybe taking more of the Rivotril for some of the Parkinson symptoms.  I don't really mind profoundly - - - because it is very minimal in terms of for example what he was using before. As long as [Patient] isn't using too much of that, I don't think we will be really concerned – albeit that we don’t want to be overly encouraging the use of any benzodiazepine.

[N19-20190228-0151]
He is off the PPi in 2018 and 2019– it didn’t anything much for the 2018 cough.

[N19-20190228-0152]
Current Status

[N19-20190228-0153]
[Patient] stopped work on Aug 28th for interpersonal reasons. He wonders was this the early stages of procyclidine seeming not to work?

[N19-20190228-0154]
[Patient] contacted [Parkinson's Nurse] our Advanced Nurse Practitioner in Parkinsons. They tried Amantadine and it didn’t do anything for him, and he had quite sad thoughts in association with that (so that Amantadine won’t be likely an option again).

[N19-20190228-0155]
They looked at the Neupro thereafter as above.

[N19-20190228-0156]
The patch was started in November of 2018 with Mr [Parkinson's Nurse].

[N19-20190228-0157]
Basically it started off and he was gradually brought up from 1 milligram through 2 and 4 and 6 and then 10 milligrams. Indeed, they went further up to about 14 and 16 milligrams.

[N19-20190228-0158]
A summary of how [Patient] would feel, having come back down to 10 milligrams is that: it feels like it's not doing enough - and it's not doing what it should be doing.

[N19-20190228-0159]
However compared with the higher doses, he prefers the lower 10 milligram dose.

[N19-20190228-0160]
In terms of the impulsivity - - He explains he’ll find himself alone with the phone, and on the phone moreso (especially at higher Neupro).

[N19-20190228-0161]
There was never a penchant for gambling, but there was online dating:

[N19-20190228-0162]
Therefore with the patches - [Patient] would put them on in the morning: It would peak in useful effect at around 6 PM, and then as it fades off he explains: that there would be a dread coming on – and this was really horrible.

[N19-20190228-0163]
He would not want to go to bed.

[N19-20190228-0164]
Then there would be the “online chatting-dating with women all over the world”.

[N19-20190228-0165]
This hasn't led to any sexually excessive behaviour.

[N19-20190228-0166]
[Parkinson's Nurse] had this risk very well highlighted in advance, and [Patient] was well aware of what might be happening and he was on the lookout for it:

[N19-20190228-0167]
As he characterises it himself, it was leading to an increased propensity for "behaviours that you wouldn't shout of, from the rooftops".

[N19-20190228-0168]
Later on as we were talking about this in terms of thinking about future options (not really just about today - but in the medium and long term) if he does feel that maybe we have over-noted/overplayed the obsessive/impulsive elements and this is probably the case because he does have excellent insight into this.

[N19-20190228-0169]
He’ll come back virtually immediately though and say: “I’ve never played as much Darts in my life” – which again makes us worried.

[N19-20190228-0170]
Bit [Patient] clarifies though that he feels this was channelling free time, and used this as a way to avoid excessive computer use. He’s actually happy about that and it was a good and productive endeavour.

[N19-20190228-0171]
In essence, back down as just the Neupro 10 milligrams all of that above is "historical"

[N19-20190228-0172]
As he characterises it though - - the useful feeling though are “artificial”. Its not that he feels perfectly normal in terms of being on the Neupro. Per his own description: It’s nearly like a small ecstacy tablet every two hours. Also it mitigates against him feeling hungry.

[N19-20190228-0173]
By it “not doing much”, what he means is that: [Patient] would have expected to feel more limber and have less cramping.  He is aware still of excessive tone. He doesn’t feel as limber as he should.  With me looking at him today: o [Patient] actually looks very normal. If anything he is minimally hyperkinetic. But he doesn’t feel loose.  As he explains though, if he was coming to this appointment in in the evenings - - - he wouldn’t look as good.

[N19-20190228-0174]
OF NOTE: Absolutely---he is quite certain he was having chorea-that is to say dyskinesia, on the higher doses of the Neupro and it was bothering the low back. He was aware of the swinging hips and leaning on one leg.

[N19-20190228-0175]
Over Christmas, at around of 12 mg of Neupro he felt excellent. However the next day he felt awful – in fairness he was flu like.

[N19-20190228-0176]
In summary he’s stable on the 10mg Neupro, and the procyclidine seems OK though he wonders does he need more of that.

[N19-20190228-0177]
The eyesight was the rate limiting step with the Kemadrin Procyclidine before. As he summarises (aside from the sight) “Kemadrin was good to me” - - - though later - - speaking about the time period off Kemadrin – he explains he wasn’t so sure about whether it was much more help as he went back on it in Jan 2019.

[N19-20190228-0178]
Impression

[N19-20190228-0179]
In essence, this can be summarised in a very simple way. It is an extremely, extremely difficult "tight rope".

[N19-20190228-0180]
[Patient] knows that he had this tendency to have the “swinging” (~Choreoform) on the higher doses of the dopamine agonist and indeed it was leading to other troublesome side effects including compulsive behaviours.

[N19-20190228-0181]
He feels less than perfect at the Neupro 10 milligram dose but not as good as he thinks he should be.

[N19-20190228-0182]
Notwithstanding that [Patient] feels "not good" today, compared with how he has appeared beforehand, in very, very simple terms: one would have no idea that he has Parkinson's just looking at him today. This is radically different from how he has appeared before.

[N19-20190228-0183]
Nevertheless, if it was later in the afternoon or evening, he would look stiffer he feels and indeed even at the moment although he is evidently quite "on" - - he feels the stiffness and tension in the shoulders.

[N19-20190228-0184]
There is a slight “throw” in his leg on the left as he is walking, but it is not major.

[N19-20190228-0185]
Alcohol is very reduced.

[N19-20190228-0186]
He is smoking but I have asked him to stop please (25 rollies – it is more than it was and the Dopamine agonist may be perpetuating this as most people with iPD find it easier to stop as Prof Healy had observed to him before).

[N19-20190228-0187]
Recommendations

[N19-20190228-0188]
As above, it is extremely difficult to navigate to a "perfect place".

[N19-20190228-0189]
I’m not so worried about deterioration times if he has a cold or flu or he is sick, with something-the Parkinson's will just automatically play up.

[N19-20190228-0190]
What I’m more bothered about is his overall Quality of Life but not disadvantaging him, or putting him at risks from the adverse effects – short medium and long term in trying to balance a difficult situation.

[N19-20190228-0191]
What [Patient] most definitely does need is a very structured regularity in his life style. That is to say, I don't really want much variation in the times of getting up each day and in the times of application of the patch. This is because it makes it very difficult to have a longer term interpretation.

[N19-20190228-0192]
Less day-to-day variation in lifecycles is what is needed. For example if he was on Sinemet and 20-30 years old - if he was varying the first dosage times by 3 hours per day, we would obviously view that in the vast majority of Parkinson's patients as a major problem.

[N19-20190228-0193]
I'm not saying he has to get up at a given time or take that patch at a given time --- but it does need to be fairly standardised to give him/us a larger overview. This is also underlined by how the procyclidine time is also varying with when he is getting up.

[N19-20190228-0194]
As I have explained to him today, whatever we do with dopamine stimulation is going to interfere with the "tight rope".

[N19-20190228-0195]
This is the balance across on one hand: a lack of movement and being stiff and being uncomfortable versus on the other hand impulsivity; compulsive behaviours; obsessionality - - and indeed as he has already experienced - chorea and worse elements of chorea-dyskinesia.

[N19-20190228-0196]
While the dopamine agonist that he is on at the current time is "artificial" it is far too simple a reduction to say that if he was given the natural product-(Sinemet or Madopar), that he would feel better and feel less "artificial in himself".

[N19-20190228-0197]
(That being said he knows form the very beginning that people do tend to prefer agents like sinement mroeso)

[N19-20190228-0198]
It is the standard view across neurology that if we were to let [Patient] have the more "natural product” now, what it would certainly accentuate (especially in one so young) the likelihood that in relatively short order that profoundly troublesome dyskinesia would emerge.

[N19-20190228-0199]
There will come a time or sooner or later Sinemet-levodopa will have to be looked at.

[N19-20190228-0200]
Maybe we will be driven to that by obsessionality issues, or whatever---but he is only 41 and therefore it is my preference to try to leave having to look at levodopa for as long as is humanly possible.  I have to be quite clear-[Patient] was not in the least bit pushing for levodopa today. He never asked for this and he did not bring this question up himself.

[N19-20190228-0201]
He knows all this from before.

[N19-20190228-0202]
He isn’t planning on going back to work right now. Of note he had been fired the day after his original discharge from Hospital. The circumstances seemed to have been less than ideal. Ideally we’d like if he could be engaged in work.

[N19-20190228-0203]
All I have asked [Patient] to do today for the moment is to try very much to regularise timings of medications please. As noted above: It's not just about regularising medication timing it is about trying to regularise lifestyle timing as well

[N19-20190228-0204]
Dear [GP],

[N19-20190228-0205]
I was delighted to see [Patient] back at neurology outpatient’s clinic today.

[N19-20190228-0206]
Everything is as detailed per the summary marked Feb 2019 just above and for brevity I won't reiterate it all.

[N19-20190228-0207]
In simple terms this is going to be a very “tightrope walk” in terms of trying to balance out a whole range of factors.

[N19-20190228-0208]
We haven't really changed anything today (there has been a lot of change in the last 4 months) and this is about trying to achieve a degree of acceptable stability in medications and management.

[N19-20190228-0209]
CLINIC NOVEMEBR 2019

[N19-20190228-0211]
Dear Aaaron,

[N19-20190228-0212]
[Patient] was seen by our team and myself at Neurology Consults in AMU on Monday 2017 07 03rd.

[N19-20190228-0213]
I won't reiterate the whole story with a long narrative history because it is very much as per the summary above.

[N19-20190228-0214]
This is really quite long though, and this is in part because some of its purpose is for supply to [Patient].

[N19-20190228-0215]
This is an unusual situation and [Patient] has had a lot of questions most understandably and therefore this is also meant to consolidate a lot of discussions which we have had. Additionally it is in a way to help (as he termed himself) “with debriefing”; as well as to set out for him a clear plan for the gradual titration onto different treatment modalities.

[N19-20190228-0216]
We think that [Patient] unfortunately has a Parkinsonism.

[N19-20190228-0217]
Ordinarily, as you can imagine most individuals are diagnosed with idiopathic Parkinson – very effectively in the community by their General Practitioner and indeed often started on medications.

[N19-20190228-0218]
In the vast majority of cases our role is really to confirm and give the diagnosis and adjust the treatments. Generally this is achieved in a relatively short outpatient visit and we adjust the person’s medications and

[N19-20190228-0219]
just observe how things are. However for very obvious reasons given his very young age at the current time and moreover the possible very young age at onset maybe 13-14 years ago, I have been keen to keep [Patient] in Hospital until we could get an MRI scan.

[N19-20190228-0220]
I'm sorry for the delays in getting this done as an inpatient because obviously he was not prioritised by Radiology as this is a long-standing issue.

[N19-20190228-0221]
However in its own way, this has all suited us and [Patient] very well. It has allowed us get a lot done with [Patient] and explore a lot of questions in what is obviously a very challenging situation. He has learned a huge amount for the last week.

[N19-20190228-0222]
Moreover, the treatment options here are somewhat more limited than usual and not as dramatically effective for the person as might usually be the case where Levodopa/Dopamine Agonists may be introduced

[N19-20190228-0223]
This longer duration of an admission has helped us have a firmer footing going forward for having a plan of how things will be introduced.

[N19-20190228-0224]
Obviously I think it is particularly clear that he has an exceptionally high cognitive baseline and he has taken on things exceedingly well.

[N19-20190228-0225]
Moreover he is well able to say when he is being overwhelmed with too much information but he will carry-over excellently day-to-day and build on what he has learned, and question very appropriately with exceptional insight.

[N19-20190228-0226]
The question of how much information is too much is very valid. However again this is a circumstance of exceptional insight. While some of the documentation may be worrying to read, e.g. the risk of dementia in some form of Parkinson’s - - quite simply very quickly anyone could find this information on the internet and I want it to be clear that we are hopeful this will not be the case.

[N19-20190228-0227]
Parkinsons? – Typical / Atypical?

[N19-20190228-0228]
We think that the cause is a Parkinson's disease. However in many respects it is of course an atypical presentation:

[N19-20190228-0229]
###lack of prominent tremor.

[N19-20190228-0230]
###?13 years already i.e. very young age of onset

[N19-20190228-0231]
### symmetry is unusual – while he has asymmetry - to a degree, his movement disorder is more prominently symmetrical than assymetrical. This can be a feature of some genetic Parkinson’s

[N19-20190228-0232]
Form of Parkinsons/Parkinsonism?

[N19-20190228-0233]
In essence, most Parkinson's disease disorders are disorders of a protein called Synuclein or how Synuclein is handled - - whereas the Parkinson's plus disorders can be disorders of protein Synculein, or range of other protein disorders e.g. very prominently protein Tau.

[N19-20190228-0234]
It is an unpleasant analogy - and of course this is a very sensitive and deeply personal subject--- however an effective practical analogy which I have heard patients come up with themselves is perhaps the nature of: "rust" and rust “spreading”.

[N19-20190228-0235]
Any individual person can conceptulise that rust may be of different forms. While most people think of iron oxide as “rust” - - we all know that other forms of rust --- depending on the underlying metal --- can develop.

[N19-20190228-0236]
In a similar way, most Parkinson's disease protein abnormalities are of synculein whereas there are other forms of protein abnormalities in some parkinsonian disorders. Additionally in other conditions there are ways indeed that the same protein can present in different individuals - producing different disease forms (e.g. synuclein disorder also causes MSA and Lewy body disease – quite separately to more ordinary Parkinson’s disease).

[N19-20190228-0237]
Obviously there is a question of whether the previously used recreational drugs may have been a factor.

[N19-20190228-0238]
I suppose it is plausible. It is possible that recreational drugs used that may have been quite harmless in the rest of the population but may have accelerated the situation in [Patient] --- however I wouldn't have any great confidence in that explanation.

[N19-20190228-0239]
The larger question is whether the formulation and chemical composition of any of the agents previously used could have caused an MPTP like effect causing his Parkinsonsism. Again I am reluctant to accept that as an explanation either.

[N19-20190228-0240]
I suspect that this was always going to happen to [Patient] and I don’t want him having any idea that this was “caused”.

[N19-20190228-0241]
Himself [Patient] was worried that the alcohol was a factor? Aside from this being somewhat effective for him as a “de-tensioning” treatment over the years, and notwithstanding that 70 units of alcohol per week can have its own consequences and would not have been helping because of how alcohol can affect DNA repair mechanisms --- I really don't give much credence to the argument that the alcohol was any major factor in

[N19-20190228-0242]
this development of a Parkinsonian disorder in [Patient].

[N19-20190228-0243]
Genetics

[N19-20190228-0244]
I have explained to him the difficulties with the genetic assessment.

[N19-20190228-0245]
One of the chief difficulties with this is the question of whether a firm genetic diagnosis will actually actively change management?

[N19-20190228-0246]
This question is (in the majority of cases) usually answered in the negative.

[N19-20190228-0247]
In a way to explain genetics, I have in a very simplistic manner broken it up into 3 different concepts: -#1 what I term for [Patient]’s understanding as “strong genetics”

[N19-20190228-0248]
---where genetic disorders run very strongly through a family. We would terms these usually Autosomal Dominant.This is clearly not the case in [Patient]'s case and therefore it is not likely that these apply. It would not apply to most individuals with Parkinson's either. It is not a worry as he has no children as yet. #2 what I term for for [Patient]’s understanding as "moderate genetics"

[N19-20190228-0249]
---I use this example to explain where a person may have a range of different genes, many of them are currently unknown but which confer “susceptibilities” to Parkinson's. Depending on the combination of susceptibilities a person has genetically, then these, and various components of environment can contribute to the development of a Parkinson's disorder. This is a theory that probably applies to most people who actually develop Parkinson's disease “sporadically” or “idiopathically” who may have susceptibility genes that we do not understand the role of as yet or known genes with variations (e.g. in the LRRK2/ SNCA / GBA genes), as well as a range of possible environmental factors. #3---what I term for [Patient]’s understanding as "weak genetics"

[N19-20190228-0250]
---by this we mean the classical autosomal recessively inherited disorders that can occur within a family but which would be very unlikely to affect other family members. It is quite plausible that [Patient] may have one of these disorders as the cause for his Parkinsons/Parkinsonisms.

[N19-20190228-0251]
More specifically given a number of issues including his very young age of onset; the akinetic rigidity; the lack of a Dominant family history; and the marked symmetry; as well as how cognitively excellent [Patient] is, there is a question of whether the indicators are that he does have a PRKN gene disorder (PARK 2)?

[N19-20190228-0252]
Nervethless aspects may point to rarer monogenic explanations such as PARK 6 or PARK7 or even PARK 9 (the neuropathy question).

[N19-20190228-0253]
A summary of where we are with Genetics can be seen from this summary from the National Institutes of health in the US:

[N19-20190228-0254]
Genetic Testing in Parkinson's Disease

[N19-20190228-0255]
Genetic testing has recently become available for the parkin and PINK1 genes. Parkin is a large gene and testing is difficult. At the current stage of understanding, testing is likely to give a meaningful result only for people who develop the condition before the age of 30 years. PINK1 appears to be a rare cause of inherited Parkinson's disease. A small percentage (~2 percent) of those developing the condition at an early age appear to carry mutations in the PINK1 gene. Genetic testing for the PARK7, SNCA and LRRK2 genes is also available.

[N19-20190228-0256]
Individuals and families who are interested in having genetic testing can learn more about their risk for Parkinson's disease and the availability and accuracy of genetic testing for this disease by setting up an appointment with a genetics health professional. Genetic professionals work as members of health care teams providing information and support to individuals or families who have genetic disorders or may be at risk for inherited conditions. Genetic professionals can discuss the risks, benefits and limitations of available genetic testing for Parkinson's disease. See: Frequently Asked Questions about Genetic Counseling

[N19-20190228-0257]
Another article on the genetics of Parkinsons written a couple of years ago from the Department here with [Senior Consultant] is enclosed within.

[N19-20190228-0258]
Accordingly the only real current value of a "genetic" diagnosis is in terms of what it might tell us about the actual neuropathological form of which neurodegenerative condition he has and maybe to some degree with prognostication and an understanding of Levodopa sensitivities? Knowledge of a particular genetic form may help with prognostication - - - but at all times we’ve emphasised to [Patient] the variability in outcomes and limitations in prognostication. We simply don’t know.

[N19-20190228-0259]
In reality, while I am not an expert on these conditions (I am not a movement disorders neurologist) --- however I suspect that whatever testing we might send may well be negative and [Patient] has been prepared for this likelihood.

[N19-20190228-0260]
Additionally there is a question of whether we should just wait and in a number of years do the whole genome or a whole exome analysis when the information underpinning the associations of various genes and various phenotypes has expanded much more from where it is now?

[N19-20190228-0261]
Therapy

[N19-20190228-0262]
Whatever form and whatever genetic cause is the cause, the question which is undoubtedly the most important is as regards therapy? Can we improve how the person is?

[N19-20190228-0263]
Ordinarily we would use levodopa or dopamine agonists however in these types of situations we tend to be extremely cautious in their usage for the reasons outlined in the summary on treatment and we will try to leave off on this for as long as possible.

[N19-20190228-0264]
No treatments are proven disease modifying, and all treatments are symptomatic – effective to varying degrees, and we will trying different symptomatic approaches with him first.

[N19-20190228-0265]
Treatment should be view in two ways:

[N19-20190228-0266]
1) Any disease modifying possibilities?

[N19-20190228-0267]
There is an unproven theoretical possible benefit giving him CoQ10 and this is started.

[N19-20190228-0268]
Azilect may be disease modifying by slowing whatever trajectory of progression a person is on.

[N19-20190228-0269]
2) Symptomatic therapy to improve his functioning and Quality of Life.

[N19-20190228-0270]
These are per the summary above and I won't reiterate all here for brevity.

[N19-20190228-0271]
However Parkinson's is much more than simply a "movement disorder".

[N19-20190228-0272]
There are multiple other domains which can be affected and which in time may need considerable assistance, and these include urological function, bowel function, sleep function, and mood-affect.

[N19-20190228-0273]
Counselling

[N19-20190228-0274]
This is an extremely difficult situation.

[N19-20190228-0275]
It is a huge amount to be taken on board – and much more than usual in more ordinary Parkinson’s disease presentations. I think it would be quite obvious that he is particularly very intelligent and he is actually got to grips with things very well.

[N19-20190228-0276]
Moreover, he has a very prescient understanding and will quite appropriately tell us when we are overwhelming with too much information.

[N19-20190228-0277]
Therefore we have tried to introduce things gradually over the last few days/week or so.

[N19-20190228-0278]
He has indicated a willingness to be informed in any clinical trials and if we're aware of anything that may be available locally, or more likely with colleagues in Dublin or the UK, and we will refer him to those services should he wish.

[N19-20190228-0279]
He will have a copy of this letter and it includes information on the rasagiline and also the procyclidine as well as certain genetic aspects of the situation.

[N19-20190228-0280]
I would ask please not to over interpret the list of adverse effects of procyclidine because many of these will have occurred in very much older individuals in himself, or in people a complex psychiatric disorders with parkinsonism is arising from the use of psychiatric agents.

[N19-20190228-0281]
He has been warned of the “nocebo” effect as well.

[N19-20190228-0282]
[Patient] has a good understanding of why we wished to delay the commencement of levodopa or dopamine agonist type treatments at least for now.

[N19-20190228-0283]
I think sooner or later we may have to introduce these. It is possible that he will do very well with levodopa and in a sustainable way whenever it is started. Indeed in time to come - we may regret that we did not start this earlier. However you will understand our delay at the moment as part of the strategy to try to make sure that [Patient] has the best possible long-term outcome.

[N19-20190228-0284]
Certainly, in no way will any individual be disadvantaged from the neuropathological or progression risk perspectives in terms of delaying commencement of the symptomatic treatments.

[N19-20190228-0285]
Plan/Recommendations:

[N19-20190228-0286]
As per above.

[N19-20190228-0287]
Follow-Up:

[N19-20190228-0288]
[Patient] has been given Mr [Parkinson's Nurse]’s contact details for any issues or questions that he has that might arise in the interim. [This is not an emergency service and [Parkinson's Nurse] is the only Parkinson's nurse in the West of Ireland. Generally he will try to get back where people leave a message within a couple of days. [Parkinson's Nurse contact details redacted]]

[N19-20190228-0289]
There is a treatment plan as set out above with gradual increase and escalation of procyclidine.

[N19-20190228-0290]
I'll write to you with any additional investigations that come available especially and most notably the isotope DATscan. We will hope use these results arriving as a trigger to review the other investigations that have been sent but are not back.

[N19-20190228-0291]
[Patient] will have a copy of this letter is well.

[N19-20190228-0292]
In 9/12 at Neurology Clinic. If your patient is not called for followup or does not receive an appointment please contact us.

[N19-20191114-0007]
Nov 2019-11-14th

[N19-20191114-0008]
(It has become the understandable preference of some of the General Practitioners to have shorter form letters and therefore all previous details are as per older letters as previously supplied)

[N19-20191114-0009]
Dear [GP],

[N19-20191114-0010]
I was delighted to see [Patient] back at neurology outpatient’s clinic today.

[N19-20191114-0011]
In Sept 2019:

[N19-20191114-0012]
We had seen [Patient] at the request of the medical assessment unit team on 17 September.

[N19-20191114-0013]
He described a hot patch on the left head and the parietal region 4-5 months and [Patient] had seen [GP] and was diagnosed with C2 problems (very likely correct – i.e. Gr Occipital neuralgia)).

[N19-20191114-0014]
However the real reason he was there in September was an acute issue with the right shoulder with right shoulder pain. By 17 September this had been ongoing for 10 days. He was not sure how it came on. However it was swiftly worsening. The pain started mild-but then it got bad. Nothing would relieve it. Lying down though was 100% effective and indeed it was still helping him by 10 days after onset. However when it would start a spasm it could take 4 or 5 minutes to settle down after he would lie down but it would settle. Sitting as a laptop, sitting to standing, playing darts: he was in big trouble. There was no obvious injury but he was wondering whether activity at masturbation was a factor. He does this lying on his back as in flat-not on his side. 9 months beforehand he had started to use a device which reduces physical intensity and usually he would not be using it with his arm - but it was a reasonable consideration. (There were separate concerns as regards the frequency of masturbation. This is because of the underlying Parkinson’s diagnosis. Some days it was not at all-other days it was between 3 and 4 times per day)

[N19-20191114-0015]
Therefore in summary there had been no injury and no fall and he felt that things were worsening.

[N19-20191114-0016]
It had started in the right posterior upper scapula as a focal point and then moved along the shoulder down under the cup of the armpit. It then would come down the arm down to the anterior bilateral elbow.

[N19-20191114-0017]
At that stage, the examination was really pretty good however he had triceps weakness at grade 4. In addition wrist extension and finger extension was about grade 5 “minus”.

[N19-20191114-0018]
Reflexes were quite symmetrically intact with essentially normal tone.

[N19-20191114-0019]
The impression of myself at that time was whether it was the shoulder or whether it was more likely a radicular problem with spasm perhaps related to a disc?

[N19-20191114-0020]
I had advised the medical assessment unit team to arrange an MRI of the cervical spine looking especially at the right-sided lower cervical roots. I also advised that an MRI shoulder would be carried out if possible. [Patient] was worried then about his ability to be still in an MRI?

[N19-20191114-0021]
Plain paracetamol intravenously had been advised to the medical assessment unit team. Non-steroidals had failed [Patient].

[N19-20191114-0022]
Current Status

[N19-20191114-0023]
The shoulder continues to give a lot of trouble.

[N19-20191114-0024]
There has been some changes in it – with some apparent settling but it is far from better.

[N19-20191114-0025]
At the General Practice, [Patient] had been commenced on neurontin gabapentin. That sort of changed the phenomenology somewhat.

[N19-20191114-0026]
I will describe this in a little bit more detail below.

[N19-20191114-0027]
The MRI had not been done in the medical assessment unit after we had seen him that day. It had already been booked and transferred to outpatient booking (but this is at an indeterminate time as an OP booking) and then he was discharged on plain paracetamol. The MRI has not happened in the interim.

[N19-20191114-0028]
Back then, [Patient] had been deeply concerned that he would not be able to stay still for the MRI. This was understandable given the amount of pain and the acute severity of the pain at the time.

[N19-20191114-0029]
However, even at the moment, he still has very significant concerns as regards whether he would be able to stay still.

[N19-20191114-0030]
This is not simply about the pain in the shoulder. It is about the general urge to move-the restlessness and the “dystonia” type elements

[N19-20191114-0031]
While he explains he would do his level best to keep still, [Patient] cannot be confident that he would be able to do so.

[N19-20191114-0032]
In fairness, as he was there sitting with me today, although he could not remotely be described as either dystonic or dyskinetic, there was a lot of movement. It wasn’t severe but it was within the descriptors of an akathesic restlessness – though mildly so.

[N19-20191114-0033]
Therefore it is easy to see and understand why there could be a lot of difficulty with him being able to sit still.

[N19-20191114-0034]
He is prepared to accept the risks of a general anaesthetic to have the MRI done.

[N19-20191114-0035]
In reality, I have to say that I have never seen anybody have an aspiration in this context, or indeed have an adverse outcome with an MRI under general anaesthesia ---- but we have to mention risks.  I have rebooked the MRI to be done under general anaesthetic please. That actually might happen sooner than the ordinary waiting list which he seems to be on.

[N19-20191114-0036]
------

[N19-20191114-0037]
[Patient] asked a very salient question as regards whether MRI is the only way to understand what is going on up in the neck and shoulder?

[N19-20191114-0038]
In reality it is not. Theoretically, neurophysiology might be helpful but as I have explained to him with an image of the brachial plexus, in reality other modalities do not approach the ability of MRI to be able to find a cause, given the complexity of that region.

[N19-20191114-0039]
Certainly it is possible that the MRI will not show anything wrong with his shoulder and will not show anything wrong with the nerve roots or the brachial plexus. But it is the best structural way we have. Neurophysiology, CT, PET, Isotope scans all have particular roles and utilities but they don’t approach the imaging ability of MRI to find the cause - - if there is a cause to be found , or if it is diagnosable based on MRI.

[N19-20191114-0040]
In point of fact, [Patient] had already explained that he does not really care about the cause-e.g. if it is a trapped nerve of the shoulder issue. However he has very very particular focused concerns as regards whether this may be a malignancy based on the experience of an acquaintance who had similar symptoms who ultimately was demonstrated to have a disseminated malignancy.

[N19-20191114-0041]
As he says, “I can deal with anything”---but [Patient] would like to know.

[N19-20191114-0042]
As I have explained to him it is highly unlikely that it is a malignancy however of course theoretically it is not impossible.

[N19-20191114-0043]
Usually if this was to be a malignancy it would have to be an apical tumour of the right lung. There is heavy smoking which is a cancer risk no matter how clear his lungs sound in the General Practice.

[N19-20191114-0044]
A Plain CXR was negative back then. That is good. However you will be well aware that of course plain x-rays are a very limited tool for excluding lung cancer. Nevertheless as was explained to [Patient], if the situation was such that there was a malignancy severe enough to be able to cause this, there is a very high probability that a chest x-ray would find it.

[N19-20191114-0045]
If there was such a malignancy that was infiltrating up into his brachial plexus in such a way as to cause this sort of issue, he should have weakness (much more than he has). In broad terms, his power is absolutely excellent. However where it falls down is at the triceps. He makes a good effort. But at best it would be called grade 4.

[N19-20191114-0046]
Also it did not hurt while I was doing it and therefore this was not some degree of give way weakness. The pattern of weakness is not really a “Parkinson’s” type phenomenon.

[N19-20191114-0047]
The way things have changed is also a sensitivity around the LEFT pectoral region. When the pain hits there is a need to exhale

[N19-20191114-0048]
=======================

[N19-20191114-0049]
He thinks there is some wasting around the triceps. There might be a bit. However predominant interest was actually whether he had any fasciculations. None are evident.

[N19-20191114-0050]
The power examination is as detailed.

[N19-20191114-0051]
This demonstrates the triceps extension power issue. This is the same as it was in September and it is quite real, and reproducible and he knows, especially form darts exactly that it is a triceps problem.  The wrist extension is actually really close to perfect. Finger extension is about grade 4+ or grade 5 minus. There is a subtle anomaly there.  He has this continuation of a patchy pins and needles paraesthesia variably through the whole of the arm and the cup of the armpit.

[N19-20191114-0052]
If he is holding the right arm in a shoulder extended position, he will have to “re-set” at the shoulder.

[N19-20191114-0053]
Other standard considerations in iPD

[N19-20191114-0054]
Sleep is actually really quite good. There are no issues there. Bowels are fine: he is going at least once every day and often 2 or 3 times per day.

[N19-20191114-0055]
In terms of exercise he is walking everyday and walking miles - however he has put on weight even in the last 2 months and I have mentioned that to [Patient] today. He can’t cycle with the shoulder. Therefore this is really badly catching him and it becomes a vicious circle.

[N19-20191114-0056]
Impression

[N19-20191114-0057]
This triceps is weak since the time of the onset of the pain. There was no obvious cause for this.

[N19-20191114-0058]
The problem is that triceps function is impaired . This could be a Radial nerve problem, or it could be in the posterior cord of the brachial plexus or more proximally in the roots / at the roots that subserve Triceps function - - for the radial: mainly C6,7,8.

[N19-20191114-0059]
It is fair to say (and he understands this actually quite accurately) that we certainly do not feel that the right arm-shoulder-triceps issue is anything to do primarily with his Parkinson’s disease. However it is the ordinary expectation that a person with Parkinson’s – they will have a more severe experience of pain than a person who would not have Parkinson’s has. Therefore I would imagine that this is quite painful disorder - but it is to be expected that his experience is worse.

[N19-20191114-0060]
In answer to his question, unfortunately, the somewhat improvement (limited) with the Neurontin gabapentin to does not give us indicators as to a likely cause. Whether it was the shoulder, or the brachial plexus, or the nerve roots, or something else completely different - any of these could potentially be improved by an anticonvulsant neurolytic drug for nerve pain - - because all pain is fundamentally mediated by nerves.

[N19-20191114-0061]
The triceps function is from the radial nerve predominantly C6-C7 and C8. However the radial nerve also has contributors from other nerve roots and therefore the element of pain and paraesthesia may not simply localise just to those defined levels.

[N19-20191114-0062]
That is why we do need to have the MRI to try to exclude any issues. This isn’t just C Spine and Shoulder – it is also brachial plexus – but while C Spine MRIs are easy to interpret, brachial Plexus and Shoulder MRIs are notoriously difficult. Negative MRIs don’t guarantee there isn’t a local issue. But a major problem is likely to be found. Conversely e.g. in a Brachial neuritis, an MRI might not show very much.

[N19-20191114-0063]
==============

[N19-20191114-0064]
The actual cause for us is pretty important.

[N19-20191114-0065]
I genuinely do not know what the cause is here. As detailed above I did and do think it is nerve root problem because of how it is positionally exacerbated.

[N19-20191114-0066]
Nevertheless, I will very often be wrong in terms of that assessment. There are a range of other possibilities.

[N19-20191114-0067]
In a worse case scenario certainly there could be an infiltrating tumour of the lung apex but I think that is highly unlikely.

[N19-20191114-0068]
Additional causes could include brachial neuritis which just arises idiopathically for no reason-it is an inflammatory disorder of the brachial plexus (expected to arise as a post infectious disorder) or sometimes idiopathically as an autoimmune disorder i.e. for no reason we can define.

[N19-20191114-0069]
Also, it is reasonable that he could have pulled a muscle in the shoulder; torn a muscle or tendon or ligament, have an entrapment plexopathy, or caused some other irritative structural issue with the brachial plexus, or that he may even have a frozen shoulder notwithstanding the triceps extension problem which does seem to localise to nerve roots/or definably the proximal radial nerve.

[N19-20191114-0070]
Therefore in reality, there is a very very wide differential – and often an answer wont’ be found.

[N19-20191114-0071]
The major part of this is for [Patient] is about trying to make sure there is nothing truly major or serious and I think its fair to summaries that that is of major interest to him – to try to make sure there is nothing serious - -even if we can’t prove a cause.

[N19-20191114-0072]
Like many others, this may then be a case of trying to symptomatically manage this – as you have tried with the Neurontin.

[N19-20191114-0073]
We have not actually given IV steroids and indeed if [Patient] wanted we could do this. It would not be unreasonable to try if we are getting no where – even as a one off “test”. The problem with that approach or “test” is of course that as a general “dogma,” steroids will make “nearly anything better”. This is because of their potent anti-inflammatory abilities.

[N19-20191114-0074]
Things may or may not improve on them - but just because they would improve, does not necessarily give us a definable answer .F or example they could help in structural causes and they could help in compressive causes and they could help in inflammatory causes.

[N19-20191114-0075]
I had wondered about bringing [Patient] into Merlin Park and getting the MRI done there and indeed giving him a round of IV steroids over just three days as a short pulse which would be / should be safe - - we however we cannot do MRIs under general anaesthesia in Merlin Park.

[N19-20191114-0076]
Nevertheless, if this isn’t settling down (and this is ongoing the best part of 2 months now) it would probably be reasonable to give him IV steroids. I have warned [Patient] that if he does want to try IV steroids (he can send me in a note if you would like to try these), he may be quite “energised or high after it ,because he has never had steroids before/

[N19-20191114-0077]
Recommendations

[N19-20191114-0078]
It is easy for us to blame Kemadrin as regards the eyesight - he is having trouble reading. Certainly it can be the Kemadrin. However he has not been as an optician in 20 months and it is time that he would be seen please. He will arrange this himself.

[N19-20191114-0079]
(We had to come back to that statement later on in a very, very extended consultation. This was to make the point that we do these letter is in real-time, which [Patient] will have at home, so that we have a shared understanding of what I mean. I do not want there to be any concern of me having a double meaning in anything we are discussing. I do NOT want it to appear that suddenly I’m blaming the possibility of presbyopia of normal age for [Patient]’s eye symptoms when it had been clear that the Kemadrin has always been a factor here in visual symptoms)

[N19-20191114-0080]
To reiterate the point about the eyes-we think that the problem here is the Kemadrin. But the issue of Presbyopia has to be considered as well allowing for his age now, and also an optician review is important as it can help exclude glucomatous or other change and for checking the intracoular pressure -in the context of the Kemadrin)

[N19-20191114-0081]
I ask for the MRI under general anaesthetic. This will be rebooked today.

[N19-20191114-0082]
He will try to stop smoking please.

[N19-20191114-0083]
I will get another CXR done . I planned it for today but with the shoulder pain, it had been done in September. A shoulder XR was done then too . This had been quite okay. There was no fracture identified.

[N19-20191114-0084]
Given chest x-ray had been quite okay in September as well. However in reality, he might leave at a couple of weeks because it is only 2 months since the last one was done. He’ll attend as a walk in e.g. Monday to Friday 0930-1230 or 1400-1600hrs Monday to Thursday – first floor main Radiology UHG..

[N19-20191114-0085]
If there was to be something there or evolving since - (as mentioned above), really it should be substantial enough to be visible on the chest x-ray even though chest x-rays have a very limited value in been able to find lung cancers

[N19-20191114-0086]
Although we happened to be having a “pill popping” talk as regards people with Parkinson’s, becoming very “dependent” on the Sinemet (in consideration of its future use) and indeed sometimes supporting impulse dyscontrol effects, I would not actually be at all worried about the amount of Neurontin he is on as such. It should NOT have such effects at all.

[N19-20191114-0087]
The total Neurontin Gabapentin dose is only 900 milligrams. It is a very low dose. Easily that could go up towards 2700 milligrams – if it is helping [Patient] with pain. Often we will be in situations of having to let people get up to 3600 milligrams. The concern he explains in the general practice was that [GP] was worried that [Patient] might fall?

[N19-20191114-0088]
This is understandable in the context of the Parkinsons gait disorder elements that are visible.

[N19-20191114-0089]
Certainly [Patient] might fall and like anybody he could get cognition side-effects from the Neurontin gabapentin. However he is tolerating it reasonably well, and he is sharp and quick today cognitively with an exceptional baseline.

[N19-20191114-0090]
Therefore in principle, short of the balance becoming obviously worse, I think he could be allowed more of gabapentin Neurontin as a gradual escalation. He will not have accumulating side effects affecting other body systems, it should not cause an impulse dyscontrol disorder --- and as long as he is tolerating it without excessive sleepiness, imbalance or other cognitive side effects : I would not have an objection to it being increased.

[N19-20191114-0091]
He knows that if it is being discontinued at should only be discontinued slowly because it is also an anticonvulsants medication.

[N19-20191114-0092]
BUT an additional issue is the interaction of gabapentinoids like Neurontin and Lyrica - with alcohol. However there has not been an obvious issue here.

[N19-20191114-0093]
I come to the alcohol separately below.

[N19-20191114-0094]
It is not my place to tell [Patient] or anyone what to do. However as an absolute, in the context of trying for weight loss - there is absolutely no need to eat to sleep. Ideally there ought no longer to be food after midnight including sandwiches and pizzas. There is some weight gain here which he is aware of .

[N19-20191114-0095]
In answer to his very valid point - - - this is NOT simply “food after midnight”. It is the lack of exercise in the context of his not being able to cycle any longer. If I could fix the shoulder pain – he’d be able to exercise and cycle and there would be weight loss. But simply put a 300-500kCal sandwich overnight, or a 400-800kcal pizza will exacerbate it.

[N19-20191114-0096]
I completely forgot to note that the Neurontin can be associated with weight gain. It doesn’t contain calories but it can contribute to wanting to eat. I apologise for forgetting to mention this earlier today – but he’ll have this letter at home.

[N19-20191114-0097]
As regards alcohol, current intake is for example 6 pints or 3 glasses of wine and 3 pints. Effectively, this is approximately every evening.

[N19-20191114-0098]
His original baseline was about 4 per night.

[N19-20191114-0099]
Technically speaking if we just assumed that this was 6 beers 7 nights a week, this is effectively 12 units per night.

[N19-20191114-0100]
It would need to be a very small glass of wine to be just 1 unit.

[N19-20191114-0101]
A single unit of wine is 100 mils. He knows that the glass of wine that he will take is a standard pub serving which is 175 mils. Effectively therefore an estimation of between 10 and 12 units per night is likely correct. This means that he is coming in at about 70-84 units per week.

[N19-20191114-0102]
I have explained to [Patient] today that that is far in excess of what Health promotion agencies regard as wise/prudent.

[N19-20191114-0103]
I have to accept his point that this is usual practice through life and it is about him having as normal a life as what he had, before all of this. It also has to be about respecting his agency, and that [Patient] like anyone - may do as they please.

[N19-20191114-0104]
There is not current evidence of damage:

[N19-20191114-0105]
---Moreover despite the falls risk, he is clearly NOT falling around with inebriation.

[N19-20191114-0106]
---Additionally it’s taken slowly and in a highly social context when he is out at darts/socialising.

[N19-20191114-0107]
---He has a frame and body habitus which means he’ll metabolise this better than others.

[N19-20191114-0108]
---Although a Radial nerve problem commonly arises in the context of alcohol (“Saturday night palsy” causing wrist drop) effectively it never causes triceps weakness as the branches come off too early – so it isn’t evidently the cause of the shoulder.

[N19-20191114-0109]
But:

[N19-20191114-0110]
---It’s c50% more than his longstanding baseline.

[N19-20191114-0111]
---It will contribute to weight.

[N19-20191114-0112]
---It is more likely to have more effect on him (e.g. in terms of balance) with the Neurontin as they act on the same receptors

[N19-20191114-0113]
---There will be likely chronic consequences to health - which along with smoking will be synergistic (in a bad way).

[N19-20191114-0114]
---He is cognitively aware of the issues and [Patient] completely understands our position in this regard, and he proposes a reasonable rebuttal of my points based on what has been normal in life for himself.

[N19-20191114-0115]
-However there is another issue here that I wanted to explore with him: Very, very deliberately, as an absolute : we would never want to have a person feel in any way embarrassed in the clinic room, in this sort of a discussion. It is pointless and counterproductive and the aim of this discussion isn’t to “force a person” to make a decision on alcohol (or smoking or anything). It’s to explain what is worrying us and give them a context for looking at this and changing- if that is what they want.

[N19-20191114-0116]
But the point I was trying to make is that he can rationalise this superbly well. It is NOT that I want [Patient] or anyone to “do what I say”. But one way or another 70-84 units is a concern

[N19-20191114-0117]
Additionally : I have to consider that a part of the reason (along with an exceptional baseline intellect) for being able to rationalise so well - can be because of his Parkinson’s disease and the medications for the management of the situation?

[N19-20191114-0118]
He does make the point that he is not sure if this is true. He certainly has very good neurochemical grounds for this argument. Quite simply: there is no more Neupro here, and therefore [Patient] isn’t on ANY dopaminergic stimulation. Therefore its hard for me to propose the increased alcohol (or night time eating), and the defence of it - as a dopamine dysregulation impulse control issue.

[N19-20191114-0119]
But I have to mention it to him as a possibility as a function of his iPD. The example I came back to is that [Patient] had earlier talked about buying 3 pairs of runners that he did not need. In a similar way as he could rationalise those 3 purchases on that given evening-I would ask [Patient] to be open to the possibility of this being a factor in him being able to rationalise the alcohol intake as effectively as he can?

[N19-20191114-0120]
He does explain why the proposition is flawed and I accept his point.

[N19-20191114-0121]
Moreover I completely accept that if he could actually cycle his bicycle, even with that intake of alcohol he would be able to lose weight very much more rapidly.

[N19-20191114-0122]
But it is still the actual amount of 70-84 units per week that is bothering me.

[N19-20191114-0123]
It would be far, far easier for me to have let the alcohol intake issue “slide”. It’s not really my business.

[N19-20191114-0124]
I really, really don’t want this to look like a situation of : we are limited in what we can do about his Parkinsons and therefore we’ll go after something else, when in reality the alcohol isn’t actually a problem - - - and that if we could do more for [Patient]’s Parkinsons and Shoulder – that is what I really should be focusing on.

[N19-20191114-0125]
Frankly reducing the alcohol back isn’t going to help the shoulder and its not going to help the Parkinsons at all.

[N19-20191114-0126]
But I’d simply ask him please to look at why there is a value to reducing this a bit please, even back to how it was before please.

[N19-20191114-0127]
I won’t address this ever again (unless Dopaminergic drugs are an evident concern as a factor in such issues in the future) and I’ll respect [Patient]’s own decision.

[N19-20191114-0128]
Follow-up:

[N19-20191114-0129]
9-10 months

[N19-20191114-0130]
I’ll write with the Imaging results as they become available.

[N19-20191114-0131]
Will review with ANP [Parkinson's Nurse] in interim if issues.

[N19-20191114-0132]
I do hope this is of assistance.

[N19-20191114-0133]
If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.

[N19-20191114-0134]
Thank you,

[N19-20191114-0135]
Yours sincerely,

[N20-20201008-0007]
Neurology Clinic – Oct 2020-10-08th

[N20-20201008-0008]
Dear [GP],

[N20-20201008-0009]
It was a pleasure to see [Patient] back at neurology outpatient’s clinic today.

[N20-20201008-0010]
In general physically [Patient] feels he is pretty good- all things considered.

[N20-20201008-0011]
He knows there is always the risk of having an injury , but physically things are OK

[N20-20201008-0012]
He can play darts and play well – and the game is good.

[N20-20201008-0013]
In terms of the shoulder/arm it can tire and fatigue but it is OK.

[N20-20201008-0014]
The severity of what he had gone through with the right shoulder this time last year is largely settled.

[N20-20201008-0015]
Liram has had a bad corn on the outside of the LEFT foot but Bunion covers have been successful

[N20-20201008-0016]
He is back on the bike which is great: [Patient] will be cycling wherever he needs to be going. Occasionally he’d be happy to head out as far as to Oranmore.

[N20-20201008-0017]
He has actually lost weight which is likely the exercise and the lack of alcohol.

[N20-20201008-0018]
As regards the Temprament. [Patient] finds himself impatient and “likely to express it”. He feels family would say that he has changed. They’d be reluctant to say he was a “bad person”. [Patient] feels he isn’t the healing presence he used to be. An example [Patient] gives in a chat with a family member wherein he was quite forthright in telling a family member about a quite worrying possibly diagnosis.

[N20-20201008-0019]

[N20-20201008-0020]
Subjectively he doesn’t feel depressed and subjectively he doesn’t feel anxious. Moreover he feels less anxious than he was before.

[N20-20201008-0021]
Cognitively he doesn’t find the brain the best.

[N20-20201008-0022]
This is a question obviously with the Kemadrin (which is also always a question with the vision).

[N20-20201008-0023]
He described for example that he couldn’t remember an appointment date for a date already set in a consultation done yesterday.

[N20-20201008-0024]
[Patient] sees a Psychologist for counselling, [GP] had wanted this done to work on insight into the “realisation” of Parkinsons.

[N20-20201008-0025]
There is a headachy element too, which he isn’t sure what it’s from. Its at night time and it is at the very least mild most nights. It feels bitemporal and frontal. He actually had trouble putting words on it. It has a jangling quality. He won’t need to close eyes, and noise is minimally bothersome at a certain volume – even low. Smells don’t bother him (Olfaction is intact and he actually can identify it as a citrus smell, but although this is a relatively strong smell for me, he did not find it “overpowering”).

[N20-20201008-0026]
Offering a list of words he wouldn’t pick throbbing or pounding but maybe pulsings. It doesn’t last more than a few minutes. There is a quality of tightness.

[N20-20201008-0027]
Therefore, although it would not last more than a few minutes, it will recurrently happen over an evening. There is no specific lateralisation - to the right or to the left.

[N20-20201008-0028]
It certainly does not waken him.

[N20-20201008-0029]
He was never a Headachy person but [Patient] was car sick as a child, quite often. His Dad used to get Migraines.

[N20-20201008-0030]
Cigarette smoking is approximately 12 or 14 rolled up cigarettes. And most of them are with some degree of cannabis mixed with tobacco. There is no family history of aneurysmal cerebral disease here.

[N20-20201008-0031]
Alcohol is nothing at all since Jan 2020.

[N20-20201008-0032]
He has been to Lanzarote a couple of times this year, and things went well.

[N20-20201008-0033]
Exam is generally stable.

[N20-20201008-0034]
There was a minimal excess of movement, but this was very, very little comparatively.

[N20-20201008-0035]
I would have difficulty in knowing which was the lateralised side. While walking initially I would have thought it was the right upper limb however there was the appearance of a “throw” in the left lower limb. We got [Patient] jogging up and down the corridor which was actually quite okay and then looking at the walk again it does become clear that the arm swing issue is actually on the left. However all-in-all, despite the symptoms he has of excessive movement in the feet especially while turning, the walk quality is actually very good.

[N20-20201008-0036]
Conversely, he is only 3 years into this diagnosis

[N20-20201008-0037]
Coming back to the foot throw which looks a little bit like a foot drop-this is actually parkinsonian because the power for dorsiflexion, plantarflexion, inversion and eversion are all normal.

[N20-20201008-0038]
Of note

[N20-20201008-0039]
He would have an exquisite sensitivity of the feet, and he’ll always want the feet covered. There is excessive movement and also urge to move the feet.

[N20-20201008-0040]
He doesn’t felt he balances great, but balance is fine in the bike – he thinks as he has momentum.

[N20-20201008-0041]
Impression

[N20-20201008-0042]
I am not sure what the headaches are.

[N20-20201008-0043]
They do not conveniently sits with any particular diagnosis. There may be a slight migrainous element but by criteria he is not young enough to be given a migraine diagnosis notwithstanding the family history. Also really, typically migraine headaches do last longer. I think we would all just be happier if I was to do a CT brain but also with the CT angiogram. There is no family history of aneurysmal disease but we will check this out.

[N20-20201008-0044]
It is probably a variant of a tension-type headache given its bilateral and episodic nature. One can never out rule that it might be coming from the Kemadrin procyclidine and the vision issues with that.

[N20-20201008-0045]
Of note compared with other things he’s always been particularly of the opinion that the Azilect has been a help.

[N20-20201008-0046]
Theoretically if in the future, and before we’d consider Sinemet (he is still only 42) if we offered him a low dose Neupro at 2mg or 4 mg again he would be interested in trying – despite everything that has gone before. Part of the problem at high dose was as per his experience an almost amphetamine quality of being stimulated in an artificial way at night.

[N20-20201008-0047]
A tiny dose of Neupro might help the RLS of the legs.

[N20-20201008-0048]
As regards the irritability the conventional thing is to offer him Escitaopram – but I’ll discuss this below.

[N20-20201008-0049]
Recommendations

[N20-20201008-0050]
Just as regards the headaches (because they are little unusual) we will actually arrange a CT brain but the real purpose of this is a CT angiogram.

[N20-20201008-0051]
This means that he will need contrast dye. That dye contrast is utterly harmless - however when they inject it he will feel warm flushing sensation surging through his body and [Patient] may also have a sensation of feeling like he is wetting himself or even passing a bowel movement. It would be gone within 30-40 seconds and there will not have been an accident.

[N20-20201008-0052]
We have had a long chat about neurodegeneration in Parkinson’s disease. I am not going to cover that all here. Basically the simple proposition is that [Patient] has a neurodegenerative disorder. A significant subset of people with Parkinson’s disease are going to get Parkinson’s disease dementia. I simply can’t guarantee this will not happen to him. He has significant worries and these are reasonable and appropriate worries. The age of onset he was at, was extremely young. It is simply absolutely and utterly impossible for us to predict with certainty his dementia risk. It would also be impossible to predict when he would get into trouble with dementia – IF EVER. At the current time I think a lot of his memory problems are from issues like the Kemadrin. Nevertheless that serves him well. The Kemadrin will never contribute to the development of a dementia state – in terms of “brain damage”.

[N20-20201008-0053]
In fact none of the medications that he is on, would be contributory to the progression of a dementia state – even if agents like Kemadrin might contribute to day-to-day awarenesses of memory problems.

[N20-20201008-0054]
Obviously the question becomes ---what can I do to reduce / limit my risk of dementia. There is no disease modifying therapies that we can offer.

[N20-20201008-0055]
The best evidence in terms of lifestyle measures for dementing illnesses obviously predominantly comes from Alzheimer’s disease. I think it is fair to say it is the generally held view that the lifestyle measures that would protect him against dementia are believed to have a significant impact, and are as follows:

[N20-20201008-0056]
A mixed varied diet with adequate fish/fish oils.

[N20-20201008-0057]
Novelty in life and engagement with new pursuits and activities.

[N20-20201008-0058]
Management of Depression/Anxiety , but there are not subjective features of either currently with [Patient], and not really other factors suggestive of that, other than this “irritability”.  Not smoking which is something that really does need to be addressed here.

[N20-20201008-0059]
The alcohol is now completely gone and that will be massively protective against this situation. I think it is fair to say that notions of “one drink per day being protective”, are not really accepted any longer. Even if there was any significant brian volume change on a current MRI we’d likely blame the alcohol as opposed to the iPD (Conversely other people can have excellent looking brains with iPD and still be very badly physically affected)

[N20-20201008-0060]
His own particular cognitive background both in terms of his intellect, and also his language based interests will be hugely neuroprotective. (the point is that he could have massive changes in brain volume over a long life and still actually score cognitively excellently on Neuropscyhological testing based on his Cognitive Reserve which without exaggeration or flattery, should be genuinely excellent)

[N20-20201008-0061]
As it happens I think it is necessary and wise to do the CT brain and the CT angiogram.

[N20-20201008-0062]
If he truly wishes to understand whether there are neurodegenerative brain volume changes already, it is an easy matter to ask for an MRI of the brain. This for example can be compared with his 2017 MRI and comment can be sought. We do not do volumetric assessments at the current time but they are a future scanning modality that will be available on an automated basis over the next decade (they are mainly at research stages now and they are not commonly done). However we can “eyeball it” and we can give him an answer if [Patient] requires it.

[N20-20201008-0063]
But it is an overly simplistic reduction to conceptulise dementing illnesses as purely a function of “brain volume”. It is actually about tract, connectivity and the loss of connections within the brain. While this can be reflected in brain volume, it is probably a poorer marker and in years to come we will probably use markers of connectivity and lack of connectivity as the real markers of dementia/predementia.

[N20-20201008-0064]
Incidentally if we did have particular neurocognitive dementing concerns about [Patient] right now, we might actually be doing a PET scan - - but I don’t think its necessary either.

[N20-20201008-0065]
Coming back to the temperament issue. I wondered whether we should consider something like an SSRI “antidepressant”. [Patient] does not feel depressed and he does not feel anxious but the question is whether the temperamental issues are an aspect of what is in essence anxiety (and I’ve explained how depression and anxiety are neurochemically viewed as different aspects or projections of the same neurochemical disorder). These symptoms would not be unreasonable in Parkinson’s disease and indeed they are common in iPD. Our standard

[N20-20201008-0066]
first treatment is often citalopram or escitalopram. I think if he is becoming more bothered by this in his terms of his interactions with others or intrinsically how [Patient] feels himself - that e.g. Lexapro would be an option. However there has recently been considerable success here with weight loss. Certainly I’d like [Patient] to do a little bit better than he is with weight loss, however I am reluctant just at the moment to suggest Lexapro, because he is on a path of reducing weight. In addition, sometimes those SSRIs/SNRIs can be associated with erectile dysfunction in men. Nevertheless, if he is becoming more bothered about temperament or family members are becoming more bothered about temperament, Lexapro would be a very reasonable thing to try?

[N20-20201008-0067]
Follow-up:

[N20-20201008-0068]
9-10 months

[N20-20201008-0069]
Will review with ANP [Parkinson's Nurse] in interim if issues.

[N20-20201008-0070]
I do hope this is of assistance.

[N20-20201008-0071]
If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.

[N20-20201008-0072]
Thank you,

[N20-20201008-0073]
Yours sincerely,

[N20-20201008-0074]
[Consultant Neurologist]

## N21 — Neurology notes 2021

[N21-20211027-0007]
Neurology Clinic – Oct 2021-10-27th

[N21-20211027-0008]
Dear [GP],

[N21-20211027-0009]
It was a pleasure to see [Patient] back at neurology outpatient’s clinic today.

[N21-20211027-0010]
Of note, [Patient] has started doing a mediation course - which is going quite well.

[N21-20211027-0011]
As regards current status, [Patient] would describe that yesterday was a particularly very good day.

[N21-20211027-0012]
Going back to September 2021, [Patient] had felt a bit undertreated. He had contacted [Parkinson's Nurse] Borwne and [Patient] was given what would be viewed in fairness as a very tiny dose of Sinemet. That was 62.5 mg twice daily (12.5/50mg BD) as the first start of a Levodopa drug.

[N21-20211027-0013]
In the context of doing the course, it is a bother to him going between the reading glasses and the distance glasses. Therefore he had made an appointment for varifocals with the optician. He had slightly high pressures apparently at the optician. [Patient] then contacted [Parkinson's Nurse] about that and [Parkinson's Nurse] suggested that we would remove the Kemadrin and replace it with the Sinemet (for the ocular pressure).

[N21-20211027-0014]
[Patient] thinks that this might allow the eyes be somewhat better. [Patient] just had another intraocular pressure test yesterday with the optician and the pressure was still a bit raised (which was 23 and 24 where 22 would be the optimal maximum, they explained and 2 years previously the level had been 18).

[N21-20211027-0015]
[Patient] is obviously hoping that the Sinemet change with the increased dose now to 4 times per day will more than compensate for the Kemadrin removal and he is tolerating the Sinemet introduction and escalate well..

[N21-20211027-0016]
The weight is fine he explains. He is not specifically dieting.

[N21-20211027-0017]
To me, he looks like he has lost a fair amount of weight. He is still carrying some central adiposity, however it is not bad compared with before.

[N21-20211027-0018]
In terms of temperament, [Patient] would feel relaxed and peaceful in himself.

[N21-20211027-0019]
Obviously one of the biggest questions with the Sinemet was what are the impulse control disorder (ICD) elements like? (In fairness most of the impulse control problems that we see tends to be more with the synthetic agents like the neupro, and the pramipexole and the ropinirole). He describes a slight intensity may be in the first 3 or 4 days of the Sinemet starting and he had noticed a bit of change.

[N21-20211027-0020]
(ICD elements can be alcohol, increased spending, surreptitious spending, feeling of lack of control, wanting to buy things, obsessional thoughts).

[N21-20211027-0021]
Nevertheless that does seem to have settled but he (unlike many people with impulse control disorders) still retains very good insight into it and I think we can depend on him. That may not always be the case and [Patient] knows this. The alcohol is still gone.

[N21-20211027-0022]
On the other side of the impulse control issues, a part of that can be that tobacco use and cannabis use were recorded; quantities are redacted as non-essential for publication. (most smokers who get Parkinsons seem to stop – likely for lack of dopminerigc reward reinforcement of tobacco)

[N21-20211027-0023]
Impression

[N21-20211027-0024]
At the current time [Patient] seems to be in a relatively good place. In many respects in life I am always telling people that we “have to live in the day”. That is obviously what is important to QoL. Nevertheless we also do have to have a responsibility to the future. He is well aware of the impulse control disorder concerns that we have with the Sinemet but the impulse control element of things seems to him to be “well under wraps”.

[N21-20211027-0025]
There is also the Chorea.

[N21-20211027-0026]
The other thing that I am a little bit concerned about is that effectively the Sinemet dose has become 62.5 mg x4 times per day rather quickly. Compared with other people with Parkinson’s who would be self increasing the Sinemet rather quickly, the circumstances here were somewhat justified:

[N21-20211027-0027]
The Kemadrin was being removed as it seemed that it needed to go.

[N21-20211027-0028]
But the thing that has to be understood here is that his effective dose of Sinemet is probably higher than other people on Sinemet 62.5 mg x4 times per day because he is actually on the Comtess already. (Conversely most Sinemet is after food)

[N21-20211027-0029]
Therefore what I am trying to articulate here is that I do not want the Sinemet rising significantly above where we are. Already he has had the slightest awareness of chorea while playing darts.

[N21-20211027-0030]
It is a general principle of Parkinson’s management all over the world that we try and spare Sinemet for as long as possible in people who are very young (and [Patient] is still just 44). Additionally when Sinemet is used, the aim should be to keep the dosing as absolutely minimal as possible.

[N21-20211027-0031]
One issue that I have to acknowledge that [Patient] is hopeful and looking forward to is that the “cognitive effects” that can happen with Kemadrin (which I have never been really worried too much about especially in [Patient]) but which he has always been aware of may improve with the removal of the Kemadrin.

[N21-20211027-0032]
I would guess that the pressure in the eyes will probably subside over the next few weeks and he can have the

[N21-20211027-0033]
eye pressure checked again. If the eye pressure is still raised then obviously it is a question of a glaucomatous situation and likely then quite independent of the Kemadrin.

[N21-20211027-0034]
He has already paid for the varifocal.

[N21-20211027-0035]
He might as well get them because one way or another, given his normal age anyway, it is expected that he would naturally be requiring distance vision glasses along with reading glasses. Nevertheless it will be interesting to see does he actually notice anything different subjectively in vision, with the apparent reduction in pressure?

[N21-20211027-0036]
[Patient] has been fully counselled on all of the above and indeed presently will have a copy of this directly at home.

[N21-20211027-0037]
He mentioned about DBS today. As I have reminded him, the DBS is only ever for the tremor element. It will not help with anything else. He will likely always remain a candidate for DBS and we can look into this ad any time if he wishes to proceed with that

[N21-20211027-0038]
Follow-up:

[N21-20211027-0039]
12 months

[N21-20211027-0040]
Will review with ANP [Parkinson's Nurse] in interim if issues.

[N21-20211027-0041]
I do hope this is of assistance.

[N21-20211027-0042]
If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.

[N21-20211027-0043]
Thank you,

[N21-20211027-0044]
Yours sincerely,

[N21-20211027-0045]
[Consultant Neurologist]

## COMP25 — Formal complaint

[COMP25-20250319-0001]
# 19 March 2025 — Formal Complaint

[COMP25-20250319-0003]
**Document title:** Formal Complaint – Diagnostic Anchoring, Bias, and Failure to Reassess (2017–2025)

[COMP25-20250319-0004]
**Source type:** primary contemporaneous patient complaint

[COMP25-20250319-0005]
**Author:** Liam Moloney

[COMP25-20250319-0006]
**Recipients:** Galway University Hospitals / HSE complaints and QPS channels

[COMP25-20250319-0007]
**Date:** 19 March 2025

[COMP25-20250319-0008]

[COMP25-20250319-0010]
