[DS17-GEN-1092]
Therapy

[DS17-GEN-1094]
Whatever form and whatever genetic cause is the cause, the question which is

[DS17-GEN-1095]
undoubtedly the most important Is as regards therapy? Can we improve how the

[DS17-GEN-1096]
person is?

[DS17-GEN-1098]
Ordinarily we would use levodopa or dopamine agonists however in these types of

[DS17-GEN-1099]
situations we tend to be extremely cautious in their usage for the reasons outlined in

[DS17-GEN-1100]
the summary on treatment. and we will try to leave off on this for as long as possible.

[DS17-GEN-1102]
No treatments are proven disease modifying, and all treatments are symptomatic -

[DS17-GEN-1103]
effective to varying degrees, and we will trying different symptomatic approaches with

[DS17-GEN-1104]
him first.

[DS17-GEN-1106]
Treatment should be view in two ways:

[DS17-GEN-1108]
1) Any disease modifying possibilities?

[DS17-GEN-1110]
There is an unproven theoretical possible benefit giving him CoQ10 and this is started.

[DS17-GEN-1111]
Azilect may be disease modifying by slowing whatever trajectory of progression a

[DS17-GEN-1112]
person is on.

[DS17-GEN-1114]
2) Symptomatic therapy to improve his functioning and Quality of Life.

[DS17-GEN-1116]
These are per the summary above and I won't reiterate all here for brevity.

[DS17-GEN-1117]
However Parkinson's is much more than simply a "movement disorder".

[DS17-GEN-1119]
There are multiple other domains which can be affected and which in time may need

[DS17-GEN-1120]
considerable assistance, and these include urological function, bowel function, sleep

[DS17-GEN-1121]
function, and mood-affect.

[DS17-GEN-1123]
Counselling

[DS17-GEN-1125]
This is an extremely difficult situation.

[DS17-GEN-1128]
It Is a huge amount to be taken on board - and much more than usual in more ordinary

[DS17-GEN-1129]
Parkinson’s disease presentations. I think it would be quite obvious that he is

[DS17-GEN-1130]
particularly very intelligent and he is actually got to grips with things very well.

[DS17-GEN-1132]

when we are overwhelming with too much information.

[DS17-GEN-1136]
so.

anything that may be available locally, or more likely with colleagues in Dublin or the

UK, and we will refer him to those services should he wish.

## MH17 — Monaghan to Healy correspondence

[MH17-GEN-0001]
# 2017 - Dr Thomas Monaghan correspondence to Prof Dan Healy (“Myself and Tim”)

[MH17-GEN-0003]
**Source type:** reproduced primary contemporaneous clinician-to-clinician correspondence

[MH17-GEN-0004]
**Author:** Dr Thomas Monaghan

[MH17-GEN-0005]
**Recipient:** Prof Dan Healy, Consultant Neurologist, Beaumont Hospital

[MH17-GEN-0006]
**Patient copied:** yes, as shown on the reproduced page

[MH17-GEN-0007]
**Date:** 2017 context; the exact date is **not visible on the reproduced page currently held in the project**

[MH17-GEN-0008]
**Provenance:** image attachment (`image.png`) located in the connected Gmail account and directly visually inspected. The image reproduces a Galway University Hospitals clinician letter/page concerning Liam Moloney.

[MH17-GEN-0009]
**Status:** faithful redacted transcription of the visible substantive correspondence. The source image remains controlling. No analytical findings are made on this page.

[MH17-GEN-0011]
---

[MH17-GEN-0013]
**CC: Prof Dan Healy, Consultant Neurologist, Beaumont Hospital, Dublin 9**

[MH17-GEN-0015]
Dear Dan,

[MH17-GEN-0017]
I wonder if I might seek your advice please as regards Mr Moloney.

[MH17-GEN-0019]
Liam is remarkable young man who progressed very far on Channel 4s Countdown in recent years.

[MH17-GEN-0021]
Unfortunately Liam seems to have had symptoms of Parkinson's for the last 13 years. He is now age 39 at current presentation, and this is his first time being seen by Neurology.

[MH17-GEN-0023]
I would be grateful for advice on what genetic testing might be appropriate in the circumstances and more particularly, whether you thing this is reasonable and likely to help Liam?

[MH17-GEN-0025]
In particular would you have genetic contacts who might analyse DNA? UHG will pay wherever the NCMG send samples of course. I have looked at Centogenes proposals as enclosed.

[MH17-GEN-0027]
I have his imaging enclosed on disc.

[MH17-GEN-0029]
If you felt it might be of benefit to him to be seen in Dublin, Liam would be happy to be seen publically or privately for a once off opinion.

[MH17-GEN-0031]
**Myself and Tim are keen to hold back on Levodopa insofar as maybe possible unless you'd advise otherwise?**

[MH17-GEN-0033]
We have made a Video which Liam had consented to for Clinical Purposes only - - - and this includes the condition of provision to Beaumont for a second opinion.

[MH17-GEN-0035]
With thanks and kind regards,

[MH17-GEN-0037]
Thomas

[MH17-GEN-0039]
---

[MH17-GEN-0041]
**Source-status note:** This reproduced contemporaneous page directly supports a consultative/collective element in the levodopa-deferral position and a request to Prof Dan Healy for genetic advice and possible second opinion. The page itself does **not identify “Tim.”** Identification of Prof Timothy Counihan as having given advice in 2017 comes from the separate 29 July 2025 Monaghan submission to the Medical Council. The exact original date/header and any surrounding pages should still be obtained if available.

## N22 — Neurology notes 2022

[N22-GEN-0007]
Neurology clinic

[N22-GEN-0008]
Dear [GP],

[N22-GEN-0009]
It was a pleasure to see [Patient] back at clinic today.

[N22-GEN-0010]
[Patient] explains he is getting on very well.

[N22-GEN-0011]
He’d bought a dog this time last year. He feels his physical activity is now much better. He is walking 2.5-3 hrs per day.

[N22-GEN-0012]
He finished his qualification as a mediator and is due to qualify.

[N22-GEN-0013]
He is starting another course presently and he will be doing a healing practitioner course.

[N22-GEN-0014]
Most mornings the feet take 20-25 mins to warm up. They are a bit sore - with the corns and calluses feeling like a “coronal pain” around the edges. He wonders is it neuralgic?

[N22-GEN-0015]
He has cramping but it is almost “zero percent”. There have been maybe just 3-4 of small finger locking crampings in the last few months.

[N22-GEN-0016]
The Meds are above.

[N22-GEN-0017]
Small Sinemets are optional. He takes some Diazepam in lieu of the missing Rivotril, but that will be back soon. He was not taking rivotril every night anyway

[N22-GEN-0018]
He has to adjust Sinemet according to expected demands e.g. for the playing darts.

[N22-GEN-0019]
In terms of other issues

[N22-GEN-0020]
He has lost a load of weight. This is with the dog and with intention.

[N22-GEN-0021]
Sleep is generally OK.

[N22-GEN-0022]
Bowels are regular and he is going every day.

[N22-GEN-0023]
Alcohol intake is about 4 pints every 2nd day and he feels this is much more under control. If he went over 4 pints he would not be able to manage it as [Patient] explained.

[N22-GEN-0024]
Impression.

[N22-GEN-0025]
Things are going well here.

[N22-GEN-0026]
Looking at [Patient] today, it is at most "subtle" in terms of excess movement. I can see it because "I am looking for it". It is not intrusive and it is not excessive. However there are very subtle elements of slightly excessive movement. In no way would it be fair to characterise this as ordinary wiggling dyskinesia but there is just that slight little excessive movement. Nevertheless, it is at a very acceptable level and it certainly is not intrusive; embarrassing nor bothersome for him.

[N22-GEN-0027]
However it has to be remembered that this is happening with a very minimal dose of levodopa built into that Stalevo and he had more difficulty with Stalevo 75 whereby he well remembers being very obviously dyskinetic (the chorea).

[N22-GEN-0028]
Recommendations.

[N22-GEN-0029]
It will only be a short period of time before Rivotril comes back available. These are supply chain issues that are plaguing medicine at the moment. He actually has lots and lots of Rivotril at home. His Rivotril usage was may be half the days per month at a maximum. In a similar way his usage of the diazepam (that he has been given to supplement the loss of the Rivotril has been pretty minimal). For technical reasons, if he wants to take a benzodiazepine at night to assist with sleep; I would really prefer it would be the Rivotril. There are a range of reasons for this. The diazepam is very short acting. I would just prefer that it would be the Rivotril that would be used as it is safer. This is only if he needs it.

[N22-GEN-0030]
We have had a long chat about how things are likely to change over time with dyskinesia. Most people will prefer the “experience” of dyskinesia as troublesome as it is , as opposed to being the frozen or akinetic unable to move.

[N22-GEN-0031]
We had a long chat about the logic in previous considerations of Sinemet usages. 50% of all people on Sinemet will have dyskinesia within 5 years. But delayed start studies and other work proves that while the Sinemet fuels or power the dyskinesia that it does not “cause” it. The cause is the impaired ability of the brain in handling normal dopamine.

[N22-GEN-0032]
While this may give more trouble in time to come with increased dopamine that may become needed - - as above most individuals will “prefer” the excessive movement over being akinetically rigid.

[N22-GEN-0033]
He had done chemistry at college and [Patient] asked about the way different chemical compounds and indeed pesticides can maybe be associated with inducing Parkinson's disease? He would have taken a smell of certain compounds at various different times. The short answer here is that it is really, really difficult to see or imagine how he could have chemically induced his own Parkinson's.

[N22-GEN-0034]
When we see it, really the only place we see it happening in usual society is very rarely certain "drug dealers" involved in the compounding of certain agents, developing this sort of Parkinsonian scenario. It is unlikely that

[N22-GEN-0035]
his is a toxic Parkinsonism. Obviously his own situation is very different to the usual drug induced Parkinson's that we also see from medicinal compounds such as in the management of schizophrenias.

[N22-GEN-0036]
[Patient] asked how far away from a cure. He will forgive me please for being "realistic". Unfortunately things are still a very long way away. Our biggest hopes and expectations had centred majorly on antibody therapy-antusynculein nuclear antibody therapy. 2 major trials were published in the summer and unfortunately these did not go well at all (Editorial from NEJM attached). Basically the treatment failed in terms of clearing the pathological substrate of Parkinson's disease – the synuclein. Nevertheless, while it is not meant to be a consolation, the very fact of the magnitude of the population that are affected by Parkinson's disease will always ensure that there will always be massive research into this on an ongoing basis.

[N22-GEN-0037]
I have not made any changes today.

[N22-GEN-0038]
Follow-up:

[N22-GEN-0039]
In 1 year Neurology Clinic.

[N22-GEN-0040]
I do hope this is of assistance.

[N22-GEN-0041]
If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.

[N22-GEN-0042]
Thank you,

[N22-GEN-0043]
Yours sincerely,

[N22-GEN-0044]
[Consultant Neurologist]

[while psychological aspects will always worsen Parkinsons e.g. in the simplest setting stress making a tremor or an embarrassing OFF situation worse; or in other settings the embarrassment of Dyskinesia making Dyskinesia worse - - -I don’t think this is some simply psychological process or Functional Neurological Disorder.]

[N23-GEN-0028]

Examination of the gait. Superficially, the gait actually looks okay though I did see some stumbles. However when the gait is watched for longer, the arm swing is actually somewhat excessive. This could be characterised as mildly choreiform as he describes himself – i.e. a Choreaform dyskinesia. However again compared with the majority of people with Parkinson's who have a history of iPD for so long, and especially so compared with most younger people on Levodopa, this is actually relatively very minimal. Conversely you could see how a little extra could easily become too much.

Darts (only played 4 times in the last 6th months)

[N23-GEN-0042]
He actually found the video that he meant: This showed him playing darts at 11 PM at night. There would have been alcohol on board and Levodopa would have been taken as Stalevo to facilitate this. His capacity at the darts while throwing was actually very very good. There were then some stumbles that looked like stumbles, but really could be nearly characterised as extra steps. Then as he comes back to standing still there was some mild excessive movement at the hips. However, compared with the vast majority of people with Parkinson's disease who have chorea, this would be considered exceptionally minimal at least in this video.

[N23-GEN-0043]
He feels this is not representative of most evening whereby he would be "crippled" with a “protective stance, lack of movement, and short steps”.

[N23-GEN-0044]

[N23-GEN-0045]

Impression

[N23-GEN-0047]
I’m nervous of certain aspects. The exercise quantum is great and really he looks fit and well.

[N23-GEN-0048]
But I’m concerns about the tobacco, but moreso about the cannabis and orders of magnitude more concerned about the alcohol at 4>5 drinks per night - - which is now on top of Lyrica at 600mg per day. It makes the interpretation of the whole situation really difficult.

[N23-GEN-0049]
I’m nervous of the diet, but it seems to be a hot meal per day with meat/veg and I’ll want him back on low dose folate 400mcirog (not the old 5mg), Vit D 800 and now I’d like thiamine 100microg. A Vit B complex with some B12 would probably be no harm too.

[N23-GEN-0052]
From the bottom of my heart nothing would give me greater joy than to see a normalised DaTSCAN. While it would be embarrassing if there was a cause other than iPD, we have a larger interest in his diagnosis being right.

[N23-GEN-0053]

[N23-GEN-0054]

[N23-GEN-0055]

[N23-GEN-0056]
I think there could be a reasonable suspicion of that most of the problem of not being able to walk is the lack of Levodopa?

[N23-GEN-0057]
The point that I am trying to make to him is that he is already taking the 2nd Stalevo on the evenings that he is going to play darts. He feels that his walking is not perfect on these evenings, but the point is that as imperfect as it is, is the Stalevo is facilitating him playing the darts?

[N23-GEN-0058]
He then makes the point that "I am taking the Stalevo for "Pep in the Step" and "for pain".

[N23-GEN-0059]
I would feel that both of these experiential points can be easily understood in the context of how levodopa works in iPD:

[N23-GEN-0060]
In the first case, the fundamental purpose of the levodopa is quite literally to give people "pep in their step". More accurately it is meant to take them from being bradykenetic (slow in movement) to having an ability move. We characterise it as fluidity of movement and taken to excess, or minimal dosing in people sensitive to Sinemet that emerges as chorea. (People who don’t need Levodopa will never get chorea) It is the question of whether some Stalevo in the evening time regularly would facilitate that 4th walk (or Madopar or Sinemet of Sinemet CR).

[N23-GEN-0061]
It is a classic and well established feature of Parkinson's disease in the last 20 years that whatever pain syndrome a person may have (classically lumbar disc pain because it happens with the older age groups than [Patient]) that when a person is "OFF" from a Parkinson's perspective that all experiences of pain are worse. This is a well-established feature called a non-motor fluctuation of Parkinsons.

[N23-GEN-0062]
I would have felt that is probably why he feels he has an improvement in the pain symptoms in the evenings where he takes the Stalevo. This probably answers why he feels he is taking the Stalevo "for pain".

[N23-GEN-0063]
To acknowledge his own feelings in this regard, [Patient] explains that he does not believe that the DaTscan if it was repeated now would be the same. In essence, what [Patient] is explaining is that: while he can’t say it whole heartedly that he believes the Parkinsons is gone, but he believes that there is a separate problem with the feeling that such a separate problem is the major part of what is going on.

[N23-GEN-0064]
He would wish the DATSCSAN to be repeated to understand whether a cure has been achived.

[N23-GEN-0065]
[Patient] and I know each other for a very long time and I think it is fair to say that we get on very well. [N23-GEN-0066]
explainable by the Parkinson's, and him having very reasonable and justifiable concerns that a lot of this relates to the feet as a separate problem. The very first symptoms were in the feet all those years ago notwithstanding that the previous “peripheral neuropathy question”, seemed to subside.

[N23-GEN-0067]
Accordingly, in addition to this and to try to make sure that we are not missing very rare entities that have significant neuropathological overlaps with idiopathic Parkinson's disease (and indeed can be a cause of Parkinson's disease), we will be checking Alpha galactosidase levels which will be going to London. This will take some months to come back from London ([Patient] will need to contact Mr [Parkinson's Nurse] the Parkinsons Disease nurse in 4 months time for this result to exclude the possibility of Fabry disease). It is simply to try to ensure that he does not have Fabry disease which can have an overlap between Parkinson's and peripheral neuropathy.

[N23-GEN-0068]
I should acknowledge that peripheral neuropathic symptoms are very very common in Parkinson's disease.

[N23-GEN-0069]

[N23-GEN-0070]

[N23-GEN-0073]

[N23-GEN-0076]

[N23-GEN-0077]

[N23-GEN-0078]

[N23-GEN-0079]

[N23-GEN-0080]

[N23-GEN-0083]
Finally, an isotope DaTscan has been booked. That scans are not routinely used for Parkinson's disease monitoring and have little clinical role. Furthermore, the Radiology Department tries to limit the amount of times we ask for DaTscans (the nuclear medicine facility has a high demand for Oncology imaging too - - - it is not simply about cost) and we have more heavily than usual asked for a number of these in the last month. They may decline an isotope DaTscan for follow-up, however I have tried to articulate the reasons to them why I think it is reasonable to proceed with this formally.

[N23-GEN-0084]
[Patient] has been fully counselled on all of the above and indeed presently he will have a copy of this at home.

[N23-GEN-0085]
I do hop this is satisfactory.

[N23-GEN-0086]
Follow-up:

[N23-GEN-0087]
In 1 year Neurology Clinic.

[N23-GEN-0088]
I do hope this is of assistance.

[N23-GEN-0089]
If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.

[N23-GEN-0090]

[N23-GEN-0091]

## N24 — Neurology notes 2024

[N24-GEN-0007]
Neurology note:

[N24-GEN-0008]
Dear [GP],

[N24-GEN-0009]
It was a pleasure to see [Patient] at clinic today.

[N24-GEN-0010]
The medication is as detailed above. This is largely similar. The Lyrica is 150 mg 4 times per day. Generally the Sinemet is on an empty stomach except for after dinner. He does not drink a lot of fluids specifically with the Sinemets, but he is drinking plenty during the day. As regards the liquid with Sinemet we tend to like a good volume of non-milky fluid being taken with this. Therefore a half a glass or a glass of the cordial that he likes, would be fine.

[N24-GEN-0011]
The Anxicalm dose is half a tablet - he is not sure how much taken at night. If and when the Rivotril becomes available, he would prefer and I think we would prefer as well that he would go back onto his old Rivotril please in lieu of the Anxicalm.

[N24-GEN-0012]
In general terms, [Patient] explains he is living within his boundaries. He’s generally well and he feels he’s functioning well within his day, but he does realise life is “a lot smaller”. E.g. it takes a long time to fold clothes - especially in the evenings - he’s very tired. Pumping tyres on the bike means that after 7 mins his toes will be curling in. He is down to darts 90 min per week down from 3 hrs per day. This is a function of the foot pain made worse by standing playing darts. The Lyrica removed the foot pain – the right foot is much better, there is still some in the left. But it isn’t relieving the toe curling in when playing darts. He is happy to walk or cycle miles. [Patient] doesn’t like wakening early in the morning (and he doesn’t like it if he wakens at night – a folded sheet will annoy him). He didn’t realise how much anxiety he had til the Lyrica. He doesn’t get moody (used get for 3-4 days as he used to have every 3-4 weeks) and he thinks it’s the Lyrica, [but also it could be from mental hygiene (and [Patient] is of course likely quite right in both respects).]. Most of the time he’s not dyskietic til after the first pint (of 4-5) at c 9PM. There was minimal dyskinesia of both hands walking but it looked close to normal.

[N24-GEN-0013]
[Patient] asked me about the Fabry testing. This had been sent last summer but unfortunately they do not send us the results directly unlike radiology and therefore I had not sent this out. I do apologise.

[N24-GEN-0014]
Basically Alpha galactosidase and the beta Hexosaminidase testing were all in the normal ranges for [Patient]. Therefore a Fabry disease is not the explanation for his Parkinson's.

[N24-GEN-0015]
Impression.

[N24-GEN-0016]
The question is whether we should change anything at the moment. Himself [Patient] would feel the medication is “spot on”.

[N24-GEN-0017]
There was no dyskinesia today.

[N24-GEN-0018]
Alcohol is 4-5 pints every night which he knows I feel is much too much.

[N24-GEN-0019]
I’d really prefer if he was on thiamine because of this.

[N24-GEN-0020]
Recommendations. I do feel that we should shift the evening c5PM Sinemet to before food with a good volume of water. (He’d doesn’t mind trying this but if its problematic he’ll go back to the way he was.) We have an option on a new COMPTESS like drug now.

[N24-GEN-0021]
The problem is that he may become excessively dyskinetic? The worry is that even on relatively minimal Sinemet (and it is minimal) we can see the mild dyskinesia in his hands when we look for it.

[N24-GEN-0022]
This is the classic “balancing problem” in terms of trying to help him in the evenings, versus, pushing him too far from a dopamine perspective and making him dyskinetic.

[N24-GEN-0023]
I’d prefer he’d been on the Thiamine please 100mg, but a family member assists with nutrition. But I don’t want him at risk of an alcohol related neuropathy. He knows I worry about the amount of alcohol.

[N24-GEN-0024]
When the Rivotril becomes available again, I’d be grateful if he could be switched back to this at night time please.

[N24-GEN-0025]
Follow-up:

[N24-GEN-0026]
In 1 year Neurology Clinic.

[N24-GEN-0027]
I do hope this is of assistance.

[N24-GEN-0028]
If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.

[N24-GEN-0029]
Thank you,

[N24-GEN-0030]
Yours sincerely,

[Consultant Neurologist]

## N25 — Neurology notes 2025

[N25-GEN-0007]
* Neurology note:

[N25-GEN-0008]
Dear [GP],

[N25-GEN-0009]
It was a pleasure to see [Patient] at clinic today.

[N25-GEN-0010]
The medication is as detailed above. The major change is a reduction in the Lyrica from the maximum 600mg/day down to a total of 50mg 3 times per day. Thiamine is not really being taken anymore but that is less likely to matter because all alcohol finished on the 07/August/2024.

[N25-GEN-0011]
The one thing that is on [Patient]'s mind is foot pain.

[N25-GEN-0012]
I have gone through the other Parkinsons first.

[N25-GEN-0013]
The complete cessation of 4 or 5 pints of Guinness each day was associated with sudden and massive constipation. This is why he is doing the AllBran. It is absolutely necessary to him. If he didn't like the AllBran, we would suggest Movicol but he is happy with this and he would really prefer to avoid Movicol.

[N25-GEN-0014]
Wiggling dyskinesia is something he doesn’t notice hugely, but he sees a household pet appeared to notice the left foot movements. In actual fact, what he is describing in the afternoon in terms of the dyskinesia is a dyskinetic walk. That spread of the feet and feeling of movement of the feet and how he mimics, this is more consistent with dyskinesia. In the examination of his walk, the dyskinesia does become mildly more prominent. He tends to hold his hands together which is probably subconscious and traps them to a degree hiding the dyskinesia and appearing to normalise the appearance of the walk

[N25-GEN-0015]
People don’t comment on it though.

[N25-GEN-0016]
Sleeping is excellent. There is no dreaming remembered.

[N25-GEN-0017]
There isn’t much tremor.

[N25-GEN-0018]
He isn’t doing the darts anymore

[N25-GEN-0019]
As he observes, being seen in the morning time in clinic is not ideal because the morning time is quite simply the best time of the day for [Patient]. He does feel "more cornered" by the Parkinsons condition especially in the evening times.

[N25-GEN-0020]
The feet (but mainly the left):

[N25-GEN-0021]
Since 2005 he simple describes “my feet disturb me”. He hobbles out of bed. He takes the meds but the feet are too sore to tie the runners. Each morning it feels like “murder in the feet”, and there is discomfort. The first 150 footsteps are torture. He feels he’s festinating but from the pain. 500-600 steps are done in Wellies over rocks on the beach. To say “I won’t live with it” allows him put words on the situation. As regards the reduction in the Lyrica, [GP] had talked about

[N25-GEN-0022]
reducing this. [Patient] wasn’t sure why. But he reduced the Lyrica himself 6 months after [GP] left it where it was (probably from August September 2024 when he start the Healthy Life approach himself). Himself [Patient] had thought that the improvements that he had psychologically were from improvements in mental hygiene. As we acknowledged last year that most certainly could have been a factor in the anxiety element improvement. Because of those improvements, [Patient] had reduced the Lyrica.

[N25-GEN-0023]
Once the Parkinsons medications hit his system he does have a relief in the feelings in the feet.

[N25-GEN-0024]
Impression.

[N25-GEN-0025]
First of all, it is an absolutely excellent result that the alcohol is completely gone.

[N25-GEN-0026]
If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here, the alcohol being gone will hopefully slow the deterioration of that.

[N25-GEN-0027]
Just so we are clear, I have gone through my own letter of last year with [Patient]. I had not actually suggested or otherwise implied that the Lyrica be reduced. Most certainly it was noted that he was at the maximum dose of this in 2024, at 150mg 4 times per day equivalent to 600mg.

[N25-GEN-0028]
I suspect that he is psychologically much better as a function of all of the mental hygiene work he has done and continues to practice.

[N25-GEN-0029]
I do not think he really needs the Lyrica from the perspective of the psychology.

[N25-GEN-0030]
He knows very well that once the Sinemet hits his system that the feet feel better from a pain perspective. That is commonly described. Sinemet should not do anything for pain but it will relieve the experience of pain to at least some degree. For example if he had had old serious ankle or leg fractures that were still playing up Sinemet would probably make them feel better as well.

[N25-GEN-0031]
However, I do think he is missing the Lyrica from the perspective of the feet?

[N25-GEN-0032]
Quite simply, pain is not really a direct part of Parkinson's at all. Certainly if person has dystonia a person can have painful dystonia. However, the type of pain that he is describing is not a direct Parkinson's phenomenology.

[N25-GEN-0033]
But I have little doubt other than that if we increased the Sinemet, he would probably get some further relief from the pain. It would not likely go away. But there would be some relief. But the problem is that if we increase his Sinemet, then he will get much more severe dyskinesias.

[N25-GEN-0034]
Therefore, increasing "Parkinson's" medications will only be of limited help and probably associated with significant complications. I do not mind trying if [Patient] would like to try that.

[N25-GEN-0035]
But an avoidance of a dose increase of Parkinsons meds is why I would suggest a consideration somewhat increasing the Lyrica. His own feeling in this regard would be, that he feels that perhaps an element of this is how bad the Parkinson's is expected to be in the morning times - because he has not had medications for example overnight.

[N25-GEN-0036]
In this regard, there would be the option on giving him a slow release Sinemet at night time?

[N25-GEN-0037]
The last Sinemet in the evening - night time at the current time is taken at about 8 PM, and bed is at 1 AM and he wakens to get up at 0845 hrs.

[N25-GEN-0038]
As he explains he would be definitely open to trying this. I cannot promise [Patient] that this is going to work. Also it may be associated with intolerable dyskinesias.Furthermore it may promote night-time dreaming.

[N25-GEN-0039]
He knows all of this from before - but he is willing to try and I do think it is very reasonable to try.

[N25-GEN-0040]
The very first thing that he would be interested in trying - and it is entirely reasonable might be to try to wake up around about 08:00am and swallows the tablets while still in bed. I didn't suggest this myself because we do not like breaking people's sleep, but in reality it is what a lot of patients will do to "get themselves going". He actually doesn't mind trying this at all because he will have woken and gone to the toilet probably before 8 anyway.

[N25-GEN-0041]
First of all, we will start Sinemet controlled release for [Patient]. He is going to take this probably around somewhere between 12 midnight and 1AM - probably as close to going to bed as

[N25-GEN-0043]
He was wondering about seeing a chronic pain specialist. It would be my own suspicion that they would probably recommend Amitriptyline or increasing the Lyrica. As he explains himself, it would be his view that at the 600mg of the Lyrica the pain was certainly not eliminated. Nevertheless, the concern is that he was doing relatively better on that then now.

[PSL-GEN-0152]
## PSL-0002 — Lyrica initiation, dose escalation, and perceived lack of material effect

[PSL-GEN-0154]
**Statement date:** 2026-08-12

[PSL-GEN-0155]
**Historical period/event described:** approximately 2023 onward

[PSL-GEN-0156]
**Statement type:** treatment received / symptom experience / patient interpretation

[PSL-GEN-0157]
**Prompt context:** Liam volunteered a clarification about how Lyrica entered his treatment history and how he understood its effect during a period of severe medication-related movement fluctuation.

[PSL-GEN-0159]
### Liam's statement

[PSL-GEN-0161]
> “Lyrica was obtained outside the treatment pathway initially. It was initiated outside the treatment pathway in approximately 2023. And at that stage, I was in severe cycles of dystonia and dyskinesia caused by Sinemet and Stalevo and Rasagiline, all being input into my system, turning dystonia into dyskinesia, peaks and troughs, peaks and troughs, dystonia, dyskinesia, ever and ongoing. The relief, the initial early relief provided by the Lyrica was merely, it was not nerve-related and it wasn't significant, as you can see from the records. I was scaled up from 25 milligrams a day to 600 milligrams a day and back down again with no change. And that's documented for the most part.”

[PSL-GEN-0163]
### Source-status note

[PSL-GEN-0165]
This entry is direct first-person evidence of Liam's recollection of how Lyrica was initially obtained, the treatment context in which he began taking it, the movement-state fluctuations he experienced, the dose range he remembers, and his own assessment that Lyrica did not materially change the underlying problem.

[PSL-GEN-0167]
The statement that the cycles were `caused by Sinemet and Stalevo and Rasagiline`, that dystonia was being turned into dyskinesia, and that the initial relief was `not nerve-related` are Liam's clinical interpretations of his experience. They should not be converted into established medical causation without comparison with the contemporaneous clinical record and, where needed, expert interpretation.

[PSL-GEN-0169]
The stated dose history from approximately 25 mg/day to 600 mg/day and back down again should be checked against prescribing and neurology records. The repository already contains later records documenting Lyrica at 600 mg/day and subsequent reduction, but this entry itself remains the source for Liam's recollection of the complete dose trajectory and his perceived lack of material change.

[PSL-GEN-0171]
### Potential corroboration / contradiction routes

[PSL-GEN-0173]
- 2023 Neurology records concerning severe foot pain, Lyrica use, Sinemet/Stalevo use, dystonia/OFF reasoning and dyskinesia;

[PSL-GEN-0174]

[PSL-GEN-0175]
- 2025 Neurology record documenting reduction of Lyrica to 50 mg three times daily and renewed discussion of foot pain;

[PSL-GEN-0176]

[PSL-GEN-0177]

[PSL-GEN-0178]
- medication lists and correspondence from the period.

[PSL-GEN-0180]
### Analytical status

[PSL-GEN-0182]
`UNASSESSED`

[PSL-GEN-0184]
This entry should be compared against the longitudinal medication record before any proposition is changed. In particular, the record should distinguish between relief of pain, persistence of toe curling/dystonia, dyskinesia, and Liam's broader assessment that changing the Lyrica dose did not materially alter the underlying problem.
